Yep. I read the ECDAT brief. 

ECDAT — Enterprise Cryptographi…

And since the backend is being built by someone else, your job is not to build “a Streamlit UI.” Your job is to build a frontend that treats the backend as a clean data source and makes the security analysis ridiculously easy to understand.

The biggest mistake would be starting with app.py and throwing charts onto the screen. That produces the classic “student project with 14 metrics and zero UX” disaster. Don't do that, motherfucker.

1. First: lock the architecture

Your frontend should have exactly one responsibility:

Take ECDAT scan data → normalize it → present it → let users investigate → export it.

Do not make your UI code depend on scanner internals.

Use this architecture:

                    ┌──────────────────────┐
                    │   Backend Scanner     │
                    │ scan_repository(path) │
                    └──────────┬───────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
                    ┌──────────────────────┐
                    │   ECDAT Data Contract │
                    │      Structure B      │
                    └──────────┬───────────┘
                               │
                    ┌──────────▼───────────┐
                    │   Frontend Adapter    │
                    │   data_loader.py      │
                    └──────────┬───────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
              ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
              │          Streamlit UI            │
              │                                  │
              │ Overview                         │
              │ CBOM                             │
              │ Artifact Details                 │
              │ Quantum Risk                     │
              │ Mosca Calculator                 │
              │ Migration Recommendations       │
              │ Import / Scan                    │
              │ Reports                          │
              └─────────────────────────────────┘

The critical part is this:

Python
Run
results = load_scan_result(...)

Everything after that should work regardless of whether the data came from:

Python
Run
sample_results.json

or eventually:

Python
Run
scan_repository(repository_path)

That separation will save your ass when the backend guy changes something.

2. Build the project structure first

Use:

ECDAT/
│
├── app.py
├── sample_results.json
├── requirements.txt
├── README.md
│
├── dashboard/
│   ├── __init__.py
│   ├── overview.py
│   ├── cbom.py
│   ├── artifact_details.py
│   ├── quantum_risk.py
│   ├── mosca.py
│   ├── recommendations.py
│   ├── scan_import.py
│   └── reports.py
│
└── utils/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── data_loader.py
    ├── risk.py
    ├── formatting.py
    └── exports.py
Why this matters

Don't create:

app.py

with 2,000 lines of:

Python
Run
if page == "...":
    ...

That thing will become a cursed archaeological site in approximately three days.

Each page owns its UI.

Each utility owns reusable logic.

app.py only orchestrates.

3. Establish the visual system BEFORE building pages

This is where you get the “beautiful” part right.

ECDAT should not look like:

💀 HACK THE PLANET 💀
████████████
CYBER ATTACK DETECTED

No neon green. No Matrix rain. No glowing skulls. No gamer nonsense.

You want:

Enterprise Security Operations aesthetic

Think:

ECDAT
Enterprise Cryptographic Discovery & Analysis
────────────────────────────────────────────

Overview
│
├── Risk posture
├── Cryptographic inventory
├── Quantum readiness
└── Migration planning
Visual characteristics

Use:

dark neutral background

slightly lighter cards

restrained accent color

white/light-gray typography

subtle borders

generous spacing

small rounded corners

compact charts

strong hierarchy

The UI should feel like an enterprise security product, not a cybersecurity YouTube thumbnail.

4. Define your design tokens

Create a single place for visual constants.

For example:

Python
Run
COLORS = {
    "background": "#0B0F14",
    "surface": "#111820",
    "surface_alt": "#151D27",
    "border": "#26313D",
    "text": "#F3F4F6",
    "muted": "#94A3B8",
    "accent": "#60A5FA",

    "critical": "#EF4444",
    "high": "#F97316",
    "medium": "#EAB308",
    "low": "#22C55E",
}

Then your entire application uses the same vocabulary.

Don't have:

Python
Run
"#ff0000"

in one file,

Python
Run
"red"

in another,

and:

Python
Run
"#e53935"

somewhere else.

That's how visual consistency gets murdered.

6. Create a global application state

Use st.session_state.

For example:

Python
Run
if "scan_result" not in st.session_state:
    st.session_state.scan_result = None

if "selected_artifact" not in st.session_state:
    st.session_state.selected_artifact = None

Then pages can share:

Python
Run
st.session_state.scan_result

without passing giant objects everywhere.

12. Don't cram recommendations into the table

This is a subtle UX point.

Your brief lists Recommendation as a CBOM column, but putting the entire recommendation paragraph into every table row will make the table disgusting.

Instead:

Algorithm | Type | Key Size | File | Line | Quantum | Mosca
RSA       | Asym | 2048     | ...  | 42   | HIGH    | HIGH

Then:

→ View artifact

and show the full recommendation in the detail view.

If the exact requirement demands the recommendation column, keep it available through an expandable/detail mechanism rather than turning the main table into a spreadsheet from hell.

14. Make evidence visually prominent

Evidence is one of ECDAT's strongest differentiators.

The backend isn't merely saying:

“RSA exists.”

It knows:

security/key_manager.py
line 42

and:

rsa.generate_private_key(...)

So make that evidence look important.

Use a monospace code block.

Example:

security/key_manager.py : 42

then:

Python
Run
rsa.generate_private_key(
    public_exponent=65537,
    key_size=2048
)

The judge immediately understands that the finding is traceable.

16. Separate quantum risk from legacy insecurity

This is very important.

Don't create:

Quantum Risk
MD5 → CRITICAL

without explaining why.

MD5 isn't critical because quantum computers are going to magically eat MD5 for breakfast.

It's already cryptographically broken.

So your UI should distinguish:

QUANTUM MIGRATION CONCERN

RSA
ECDSA
ECDH
DH

versus:

LEGACY / ALREADY INSECURE

MD5
DES
SHA-1

Your brief specifically asks for this distinction. 

ECDAT — Enterprise Cryptographi…

That distinction will make the project look considerably more technically mature.

17. Mosca Calculator should be a killer demo

This page should be interactive.

Layout:

Migration Timing Analysis

┌───────────────────────────────┐
│ Confidentiality lifetime      │
│ [ 12 ] years                  │
│                               │
│ Migration time                │
│ [ 4 ] years                   │
│                               │
│ Quantum horizon               │
│ [ 10 ] years                  │
│                               │
│ Business criticality          │
│ [ High ▾ ]                    │
└───────────────────────────────┘

              ↓

      X + Y = 12 + 4 = 16

              ↓

        16 > 10

       HIGH URGENCY

Then show the actual reasoning.

The formula from the brief is:

X + Y > Z

where:

X = confidentiality lifetime

Y = migration time

Z = assumed quantum horizon. 

ECDAT — Enterprise Cryptographi…

Do not call Z:

Quantum Computer Arrival Date

Call it:

Planning Horizon / Assumed Quantum Horizon

because that's what it actually is.

25. Make the executive summary deterministic

Don't use an AI API.

Build a function:

Python
Run
def generate_executive_summary(result):
    ...

It can calculate:

total_files
total_artifacts
critical
high
quantum_vulnerable
top_algorithms

Then produce the sentence.

Example:

7 cryptographic artifacts were discovered across
18 files. 1 artifact is classified as critical
and 3 as high risk. Public-key algorithms represent
the primary post-quantum migration concern.

No API.

No hallucinations.

Same input = same output.

26. Build reusable components

Create functions such as:

Python
Run

Then your pages become readable.

Instead of:

Python

That's maintainability.

27. Handle empty states properly

This is something amateur dashboards completely ignore.

Suppose there are no artifacts.

Don't show:

No data

Instead:

No cryptographic artifacts discovered

The scanner did not identify any supported
cryptographic usage in the current dataset.

Try importing another scan result.

If no Mosca data:

Mosca assessment unavailable

The current scan does not contain enough
information to calculate a repository-level
migration timing assessment.

If no recommendation:

Recommendation unavailable

No migration recommendation was supplied
by the analysis backend.

That is what robust software looks like.

28. Use progressive disclosure

This is the trick for making an information-dense cybersecurity dashboard readable.

Don't show everything at once.

Level 1
RSA
HIGH
Level 2

Click it:

RSA
Asymmetric
2048 bit
security/key_manager.py:42
Level 3

Expand:

Evidence
Risk reasoning
Mosca assessment
Recommendation

This prevents the UI from becoming a giant wall of security jargon.

29. Use consistent page anatomy

Every page should follow roughly:

PAGE TITLE
Short explanation

────────────────────────

KEY METRICS / CONTEXT

────────────────────────

SECONDARY INFORMATION

Don't have one page with:

Title → chart → text → 17 cards → table → chart

and another with:

Title → giant table

Consistency is what makes the app feel designed.

30. Don't overuse Plotly

Use charts where they answer something.

Good:

Risk distribution

Because the user wants to understand:

How much risk?

Good:

Algorithm distribution

Because it answers:

What cryptography exists?

Potentially useful:

Risk by crypto type

Maybe.

Bad:

3D rotating cryptographic risk globe

Congratulations, you've invented a chart that tells nobody anything.

32. Build navigation around questions

This is the UX philosophy I'd use:

Page	Question answered

That's a coherent product.

Not just eight Streamlit pages duct-taped together.

Before you show anyone, test these datasets.

    "low": 0

Dataset 3 — Missing fields

Test:

JSON
{
  "algorithm": "RSA",
  "type": "Asymmetric",
  "key_size": null,
  "mode": null,
  "file": "test.py",
  "line": null,
  "evidence": null,

Not detected

Line
Not available

Evidence
No evidence supplied
Dataset 4 — Malformed JSON

Your UI should say:

Unable to load scan result

The uploaded file is not valid JSON.

Not:

KeyError: 'artifacts'

Nobody wants to watch Python have a nervous breakdown during an SIH demo.

36. The golden rule for your frontend

Every screen should answer one of these:

WHAT?
WHERE?
WHY?
HOW BAD?
WHEN?
WHAT NEXT?

ECDAT maps beautifully:

WHAT?
→ Cryptographic Inventory

WHERE?
→ Artifact Details

WHY?
→ Risk explanation + evidence

HOW BAD?
→ Quantum Risk

WHEN?
→ Mosca Calculator

WHAT NEXT?
→ Migration Recommendations

That's your product architecture right there.

Your immediate next move

Don't start coding the pretty shit yet.

Build these five things first:

1. sample_results.json
2. data_loader.py
3. risk.py
4. formatting.py
5. app.py + sidebar skeleton

Then implement Overview → CBOM → Artifact Details.

Once those three screens are solid, the rest becomes straightforward instead of you wrestling with a 900-line Streamlit motherfucker that nobody can maintain.

And one more thing: keep the backend contract sacred. The frontend should consume Structure B. If the backend guy changes scanner internals, your UI shouldn't give a damn. That separation is what will make this actually maintainable.

Sources

Yes. That changes the frontend plan in an important way.

You don't just want an interface that looks like CrowdStrike/Wiz/Splunk. You want an interface designed with the same security-product principles: dense but readable, strong trust boundaries, controlled rendering, least privilege, safe input handling, minimal third-party dependencies, and explicit security states.

And because you're using Streamlit, there is one correction to make: you don't have the same low-level control over the browser as a React/Next.js application. So don't pretend you can bolt every enterprise-web security control directly into Streamlit. You secure what you control, and where Streamlit's control ends, you use its configuration/reverse-proxy/deployment controls.

The ECDAT frontend security architecture

I'd structure it like this:

                         USER
                           │
                           ▼
                 ┌───────────────────┐
                 │   HTTPS / Proxy   │
                 │ TLS + Headers     │
                 │ Rate Limiting     │
                 └─────────┬─────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
                 ┌───────────────────┐
                 │    Streamlit UI   │
                 │                   │
                 │ Authentication    │
                 │ Authorization     │
                 │ Input validation  │
                 │ Safe rendering    │
                 │ Session isolation │
                 └─────────┬─────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
                 ┌───────────────────┐
                 │ Frontend Adapter  │
                 │                   │
                 │ JSON validation   │
                 │ Schema validation │
                 │ Normalization     │
                 └─────────┬─────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
                 ┌───────────────────┐
                 │ Backend Scanner   │
                 │                   │
                 │ Repository scan   │
                 │ Crypto discovery  │
                 └───────────────────┘

The key idea:

The frontend is a security boundary, not just a pretty window into the backend.

1. Take the six products as UX inspiration — not code

Your references actually give you six useful design principles.

Product	Borrow this
CrowdStrike	Clear threat severity + investigation flow
Darktrace	Visual relationships and contextual risk
SentinelOne	Dense but extremely structured tables
Palo Alto Cortex	SOC-style information hierarchy
Splunk	Modular dashboards and filtering
Wiz	Graph-style relationships between assets

Don't copy their interfaces.

Build ECDAT's own visual language using those principles.

For ECDAT specifically:

CrowdStrike
    ↓
Risk investigation

SentinelOne
    ↓
Artifact evidence

Splunk
    ↓
Inventory/filtering

Wiz
    ↓
Crypto → file → purpose → risk relationships

Darktrace
    ↓
Visual risk relationships

Cortex
    ↓
Enterprise SOC presentation

That's the right synthesis.

2. Security starts with the browser boundary

Your first rule:

Never trust anything coming from the browser.

That includes:

repository paths

uploaded filenames

search strings

filter values

query parameters

artifact IDs

anything passed through st.session_state

imported JSON

backend results

OWASP explicitly recommends framework protections, output encoding/sanitization and safe rendering rather than relying on one magical defense. 
OWASP Cheat Sheet Series
+1

3. XSS: this one matters for ECDAT

Your scanner literally displays things like:

file
evidence
risk_reason
recommendation

Those are potentially untrusted strings.

Imagine the scanner finds this in a source file:

Python
Run
"<script>alert('owned')</script>"

If your UI blindly renders that as HTML, congratulations, your cryptographic scanner has become an XSS delivery mechanism.

Don't do:

Python
Run
st.markdown(user_supplied_content, unsafe_allow_html=True)

unless you have an actual reason and have sanitized/controlled the content.

Prefer:

Python
Run
st.code(evidence)

for source evidence.

And:

Python
Run
st.write(risk_reason)

for normal text.

Especially important

Don't use unsafe_allow_html=True throughout the project just because you want rounded cards.

That's the Streamlit equivalent of giving every UI component a loaded shotgun because CSS was inconvenient.

Use controlled CSS sparingly and keep untrusted data out of HTML contexts.

OWASP specifically recommends contextual output encoding and safe sinks for XSS prevention. 
OWASP Cheat Sheet Series

4. Your evidence viewer should be a security component

Make:

utils/
    safe_render.py

and have one controlled path for displaying scanner evidence.

For example conceptually:

Python
Run
def render_evidence(evidence: str):
    if not evidence:
        st.caption("No evidence supplied")
        return

    st.code(str(evidence), language="python")

Now every artifact uses the same safe rendering mechanism.

Don't scatter:

Python
Run
st.markdown(...)

all over the application.

5. Don't let repository paths become arbitrary file access

This is a big one because your future backend will accept:

Repository path

Suppose someone enters:

C:\Users\...\secrets

or:

../../something

Your frontend should not casually pass arbitrary paths to a privileged scanner.

Define a trust boundary:

USER INPUT
    ↓
Validate path
    ↓
Resolve absolute path
    ↓
Check allowed workspace
    ↓
Check existence/type
    ↓
Backend scanner

For example:

Allowed workspace:

ECDAT/
└── scan_targets/
    ├── repo1/
    └── repo2/

Then only allow scanning beneath that directory.

That's much safer than:

Python
Run
scan_repository(user_entered_path)

which is basically saying:

“Sure, type a path and let's see what happens.”

6. Uploaded JSON needs validation

Your sample_results.json isn't a security problem.

User-uploaded JSON absolutely can be.

Don't assume:

JSON
{
  "artifacts": []
}

is always valid.

Create a schema.

Something like:

ScanResult
├── target: string
├── total_files_scanned: integer
├── total_artifacts: integer
├── risk_summary: object
└── artifacts: array

Each artifact:

Artifact
├── algorithm: string | null
├── type: string | null
├── key_size: integer | null
├── mode: string | null
├── file: string | null
├── line: integer | null
├── evidence: string | null
├── quantum_risk: enum
├── risk_reason: string | null
├── mosca_risk: enum | null
└── recommendation: string | null

Then reject malformed input cleanly.

7. Don't let imported data control your UI

This is another important design principle.

Suppose JSON says:

JSON
{
    "quantum_risk": "DROP_DATABASE"
}

Your UI shouldn't blindly trust it.

Normalize:

Python
Run
ALLOWED_RISKS = {
    "CRITICAL",
    "HIGH",
    "MEDIUM",
    "LOW"
}

Then:

Python
Run
if value not in ALLOWED_RISKS:
    value = "UNKNOWN"

And display:

UNKNOWN

rather than allowing arbitrary values to become UI semantics.

8. Authentication: don't build a fake login

For the prototype:

Local SIH demo
    ↓
No unnecessary authentication complexity

If you later deploy ECDAT for an enterprise environment:

User
 ↓
OIDC / Enterprise Identity Provider
 ↓
Streamlit
 ↓
Role
 ↓
Allowed actions

Current Streamlit supports OIDC authentication through st.login(), and its documentation explicitly distinguishes authentication from authorization. 
Streamlit Docs
+1

That distinction matters.

Authentication:

Who are you?

Authorization:

What are you allowed to do?

Don't confuse them.

9. Add roles to the UI architecture now

Even if you don't implement authentication yet, design around roles.

For example:

Security Analyst
Overview
Inventory
Artifacts
Quantum Risk
Mosca
Recommendations
Reports
Security Manager
Overview
Risk
Recommendations
Reports
Administrator
Everything
+
Scan/import configuration

Your current prototype can simply use:

Python
Run
role = "analyst"

internally.

Later, the identity provider supplies the role.

That means you don't have to rewrite your navigation architecture when authentication arrives.

10. The UI itself should communicate trust

This is where your CrowdStrike/Cortex inspiration becomes useful.

Every important dataset should have a visible source indicator:

DATA SOURCE

Repository Scan
demo_repo
18 files
7 artifacts
Generated: 18:42:31

For uploaded results:

DATA SOURCE

Imported Scan
sample_results.json
Imported: 18:42:31

That prevents the user from confusing:

“real scanner output”

with:

“sample/demo data.”

That's especially important for your SIH demonstration.

11. Add a "Prototype / Live" status

Top-right or sidebar:

● DEMO DATA

when sample data is loaded.

And eventually:

● LIVE SCAN

when the backend is actually connected.

Do not make the sample dataset look like an active enterprise scan.

That's deceptive UX.

12. Add an evidence trust hierarchy

Every finding should have something like:

CONFIDENCE / EVIDENCE

Source detected
✓ File
✓ Line
✓ Code evidence
✓ Algorithm identified

For example:

RSA

Evidence quality
████████████  High

security/key_manager.py
Line 42
rsa.generate_private_key(...)

If evidence is incomplete:

Evidence quality
██████░░░░░░  Partial

File identified
Algorithm identified
Line unavailable

That is much more enterprise-grade than pretending every scanner result has equal certainty.

Important: only implement this if the backend provides enough information to justify it. Don't invent confidence scores.

13. Don't make the UI "attackable" through custom HTML

This deserves its own rule:

Avoid
Python
Run
unsafe_allow_html=True

as a general styling strategy.

Prefer
Python
Run
st.container()
st.columns()
st.metric()
st.dataframe()
st.code()
st.expander()
st.tabs()
st.caption()

and limited controlled CSS.

If you need a custom component, isolate it.

Your UI should have:

components/
    metric_card.py
    risk_badge.py
    evidence_viewer.py
    artifact_card.py

rather than one enormous HTML blob.

14. CSP belongs at the deployment layer

Your screenshot mentions CSP, and yes, CSP is useful.

But don't misunderstand it.

CSP is defense-in-depth, not:

“I enabled CSP, therefore XSS doesn't exist.”

OWASP explicitly says CSP should supplement proper XSS prevention rather than replace it. 
OWASP Cheat Sheet Series
+1

For production, I'd put ECDAT behind a reverse proxy:

Internet / Enterprise Network
             │
             ▼
       Nginx / Apache
             │
      Security headers
             │
             ▼
         Streamlit

Then configure headers such as:

Content-Security-Policy
X-Content-Type-Options
Referrer-Policy
Permissions-Policy
Strict-Transport-Security

The exact CSP must be tested against Streamlit's runtime requirements. Don't copy some random default-src 'none' policy from Stack Overflow and then wonder why the entire app becomes a beautiful blank rectangle.

15. Clickjacking protection

Your UI should not casually be embeddable inside another malicious website.

CSP's frame-ancestors is the modern control for this, and OWASP discusses it as the preferred defense against framing attacks. 
OWASP Cheat Sheet Series

For a standalone enterprise ECDAT deployment:

frame-ancestors 'none'

is a sensible target if you don't need embedding.

If later you intentionally embed ECDAT somewhere, change the policy deliberately.

16. CSRF / XSRF

Streamlit already provides XSRF/CORS configuration.

Current Streamlit configuration documents:

TOML
[server]
enableCORS = true
enableXsrfProtection = true
xsrfCookieSameSite = "lax"

as security controls, with XSRF and CORS enabled by default in current configuration documentation. 
Streamlit Docs
+1

Do not disable them just because some tutorial says you need to.

And if authentication is configured through Streamlit's secrets, Streamlit automatically enables CORS/XSRF protection. 
Streamlit Docs

17. Restrict allowed hosts

This is another one people ignore.

Streamlit supports:

TOML
[server]
allowedHosts = [
    "localhost",
    "ecdat.internal.example"
]

rather than accepting arbitrary host headers.

The current Streamlit docs specifically describe allowedHosts as protection against DNS-rebinding-related issues. 
Streamlit Docs

For local development:

TOML
allowedHosts = ["localhost"]

For enterprise:

ecdat.internal.company

plus whatever internal hostname you deliberately support.

18. HTTPS is mandatory outside localhost

For your SIH laptop:

localhost

Fine.

For an actual enterprise deployment:

Browser
   ↓ HTTPS
Reverse Proxy
   ↓
Streamlit

Never expose sensitive scan results over plain HTTP.

Streamlit's own hosted environment uses HTTPS/HSTS, but your self-hosted ECDAT deployment needs its own TLS architecture. 
Streamlit Docs

19. Secrets NEVER go into frontend code

No:

Python
Run
API_KEY = "sk-...."

No:

Python
Run
PASSWORD = "admin123"

No:

secrets.json

committed to Git.

Streamlit provides secrets.toml specifically for this kind of configuration, and its documentation recommends keeping secrets outside source control. 
Streamlit Docs
+1

For your current prototype:

No secrets required.

Excellent.

Keep it that way.

20. Don't load external JavaScript/CDNs unless necessary

This is a major architectural advantage.

Your requirement says:

No external API.

I'd go further:

No unnecessary external frontend dependencies.

Avoid:

random CDN
random JavaScript widget
random analytics
random font service
random icon library

because every external resource becomes another dependency/trust relationship.

For an enterprise cybersecurity product:

ECDAT
├── Streamlit
├── Plotly
└── Python libraries

is beautiful.

Minimal attack surface.

21. Third-party libraries need a security gate

Your reference image explicitly mentions:

Auditing of 3rd-party libraries in use

Absolutely.

Your frontend should have:

requirements.txt

with only necessary dependencies.

Then periodically run:

pip-audit

and:

bandit

and ideally:

ruff

The important point:

The frontend itself becomes something you scan.

Very meta.

ECDAT should be capable of discovering crypto risk while not accidentally shipping a prehistoric vulnerable dependency itself.

22. Add dependency security to the project workflow

Your repository should eventually look like:

.github/
└── workflows/
    └── security.yml

Pipeline:

Push
 │
 ├── Unit tests
 │
 ├── Ruff
 │
 ├── Bandit
 │
 ├── pip-audit
 │
 └── Build

No need to make this part of the Streamlit UI yet.

But architect the project so it can be added cleanly.

23. File upload security

Your Import page is an attack surface.

The upload flow should be:

Upload
  ↓
Size limit
  ↓
Extension check
  ↓
JSON parse
  ↓
Schema validation
  ↓
Normalize
  ↓
Store only in memory/session
  ↓
Render

Don't:

upload
 ↓
save anywhere
 ↓
execute something

And definitely don't allow uploaded JSON to become executable HTML.

24. Search/filter inputs should be treated as untrusted

Suppose someone searches:

<script>alert(1)</script>

It should simply be a string.

Your filter:

Python
Run
filtered = df[
    df["file"].str.contains(search_term, regex=False, na=False)
]

Notice:

regex=False

unless you intentionally want regex.

Otherwise you've created an unnecessary regex injection / ReDoS-shaped headache.

For a simple search box:

Treat search as plain text.

25. Don't expose backend exceptions

Never show:

FileNotFoundError:
C:\Users\CJ\...
scanner.py line 193

to users.

Instead:

Scan failed

The repository could not be scanned.

Error ID: SCAN-7F21

Then log the actual exception server-side.

This prevents leaking:

filesystem paths

usernames

internal module structure

credentials accidentally present in exceptions

backend implementation details

26. Build a proper error model

Create:

utils/errors.py

with categories:

INPUT_ERROR
IMPORT_ERROR
VALIDATION_ERROR
SCAN_ERROR
BACKEND_ERROR
EXPORT_ERROR
AUTH_ERROR

UI:

⚠ Import failed

The uploaded file does not match the ECDAT
scan-result schema.

Expected:
Structure B

Error ID: IMP-1042

That looks like an actual product.

Not:

JSONDecodeError: Expecting property name...
27. Session isolation

Be careful with:

Python
Run
@st.cache_data

and anything that could accidentally share sensitive scan results between users.

Your architecture should distinguish:

GLOBAL CACHE

from:

USER SESSION

For sensitive enterprise scan data, don't casually cache entire scan results globally.

Use session state for user-specific imported results where appropriate.

The goal:

User A
  ↓
Repo A results

User B
  ↓
Repo B results

NEVER:

User B → Repo A
28. Don't put sensitive scan data into URLs

Avoid designs like:

/ecdat?file=customer_secret_repo.py

URLs leak into:

browser history

logs

screenshots

referrers

monitoring systems

Use internal session state / selection IDs instead.

29. Your UI should visually distinguish dangerous actions

For example:

Export

Normal:

[ Export CBOM ]
Delete scan

Danger:

[ Delete Scan ]
Run repository scan

Important:

[ Start Scan ]

And confirmation where necessary.

Never make:

Delete everything

a button next to:

Export

with identical styling.

30. Add a security status panel

This would fit your Cortex/SOC aesthetic beautifully.

Sidebar:

SYSTEM STATUS

● Application      Healthy
● Backend          Connected
● Data source      demo_repo
● TLS              Active
● XSRF             Enabled
● CORS             Restricted

────────────────

ECDAT v0.1
SIH26-26164

But be careful:

Only show statuses you can actually determine.

If you cannot programmatically verify TLS, don't put:

TLS ✓

there.

Use:

Deployment
Local

instead.

Never manufacture security telemetry.

31. Your Overview can become a mini SOC

Here's where your inspiration from those products gets really useful.

Instead of:

7 artifacts

build:

CRYPTOGRAPHIC SECURITY POSTURE

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                 │
│  CRITICAL     HIGH       MEDIUM       LOW       │

That's your SentinelOne + Cortex inspiration.

32. Then give it a Wiz-style relationship view

This is where ECDAT can actually have its own visual identity.

Don't make a generic network graph.

Make a:

Cryptographic Dependency Map
                demo_repo
                    │
          ┌─────────┼─────────┐
          │         │         │
       auth.py   security/   network/
          │         │         │
       SHA-256     RSA       TLS
                    │         │
                    ▼         ▼
                 HIGH       MEDIUM
                    │
                    ▼
                 Migration
                    │
             ┌──────┴──────┐
             │             │
          ML-KEM        Hybrid

That answers:

Where does this cryptography live, and what does it affect?

That's much more valuable than a random 3D globe.

33. But don't make the graph the primary UI

Important.

Graph:

secondary investigation tool

Table:

primary inventory

Because when you have:

5,000 cryptographic artifacts

a giant node graph becomes:

congratulations, you've built a bowl of spaghetti.

Use the graph for:

relationships

selected artifact

affected files

migration path

cryptographic purpose

Keep the table for serious analysis.

34. The final ECDAT visual language

I'd define it as:

Base
Dark enterprise SOC
Primary accent
Cool blue
Risk
CRITICAL → red
HIGH     → orange
MEDIUM   → amber
LOW      → green
Information
Evidence → blue
Metadata → gray
Actions  → blue
Danger   → red
Typography
Inter / system sans
Evidence
Monospace
Shapes
6–10px radius
thin borders
minimal shadows
Animation

Almost none.

Use animation only for:

loading
state transition
graph interaction

Not:

everything glowing

Cybersecurity software doesn't need to scream to prove it's secure.

36. Your .streamlit/config.toml

At minimum, I would design around the current Streamlit security configuration rather than disabling protections. Current Streamlit documentation lists CORS and XSRF protection and supports host allowlisting. 
Streamlit Docs
+1

For local development, something along these lines:

TOML
[server]
enableCORS = true
enableXsrfProtection = true
xsrfCookieSameSite = "lax"
allowedHosts = ["localhost"]

[browser]
gatherUsageStats = false

Then production configuration should be adapted to the actual hostname/proxy architecture.

Don't blindly copy this into production because the correct allowedHosts, proxy behavior, and external origin depend on how you're deploying ECDAT.

37. Your security checklist

Before calling the frontend "done":

Input
[ ] JSON schema validation
[ ] File upload limits
[ ] Repository path validation
[ ] Search treated as plain text
[ ] Enum validation
[ ] Integer/range validation
Rendering
[ ] No unnecessary unsafe_allow_html
[ ] Evidence rendered as code/text
[ ] Untrusted strings never interpreted as HTML
[ ] No arbitrary JavaScript
[ ] No unnecessary external CDN resources
Network
[ ] HTTPS in production
[ ] XSRF enabled
[ ] CORS restricted
[ ] Host allowlist
[ ] Security headers
[ ] CSP at deployment layer
[ ] Clickjacking protection
Authentication
[ ] OIDC when enterprise deployment requires it
[ ] Role model
[ ] Authorization separate from authentication
[ ] Session isolation
Data
[ ] Secrets outside source code
[ ] No sensitive data in URLs
[ ] No global sensitive cache
[ ] Controlled error messages
[ ] No filesystem path leakage
Dependencies
[ ] Minimal requirements.txt
[ ] pip-audit
[ ] Bandit
[ ] Ruff
[ ] Dependency updates

The key design principle

Don't build:

“a secure-looking cybersecurity dashboard.”

Build:

“a cybersecurity dashboard whose UI architecture itself follows security principles.”

That's the difference.

The visual references you gave are useful for information density, investigation workflows, relationship visualization and enterprise presentation. The actual security layer should come from things like safe rendering, validation, XSRF/CORS configuration, authentication/authorization, secrets management, HTTPS, CSP/security headers and dependency auditing. OWASP's guidance supports treating CSP and similar browser controls as defense-in-depth rather than substitutes for safe application coding. 
OWASP Cheat Sheet Series
+2
OWASP Cheat Sheet Series
+2

And for ECDAT specifically, I'd make “evidence → risk → impact → migration” the core interaction pattern. That's your product's identity. CrowdStrike can keep its attack timeline; Wiz can keep its cloud graph. Your motherfucker is going to show exact cryptographic evidence and why that evidence creates migration risk.

Alright. Now we're designing ECDAT as a product, not as a SOC clone.

The references are only inspiration for discipline and polish. ECDAT should have its own identity: black/navy foundation, yellow intelligence accent, editorial typography, quiet motion, lots of breathing room, and a very controlled interface.

And the backend contract stays exactly as specified in the MD: the frontend consumes the scan-result structure, handles nulls, shows source evidence, quantum risk, Mosca risk, recommendations, CBOM, and the prototype/roadmap honestly.

key words in hero typography

2. Hero typography

This is where your font idea becomes excellent.

I'd use three typography personalities.

Display

Space Grotesk

For:

ECDAT

Cryptographic
Visibility.

Strong, geometric, technical.

               without the noise.

5. The breathing animation

Animation

    gridBreath 12s ease-in-out infinite alternate;

The motion should be:

slow → barely perceptible → continuous

Not:

30. The design mantra

Lock this into your head before writing the first line of frontend code:

Black gives ECDAT authority.
Yellow gives it identity.
Space Grotesk gives it structure.
Serif italic gives it personality.
JetBrains Mono gives it technical credibility.
Dots give the entry screen atmosphere.
Evidence gives the product credibility.
Whitespace keeps the whole thing usable.

And most importantly:

Do not copy CrowdStrike, Darktrace, SentinelOne, Cortex, Splunk or Wiz.

Take their discipline, not their skin.

ECDAT should feel like its own product: quiet, precise, editorial, technical, and slightly intimidating without screaming “CYBER SECURITY.”

That's the direction I'd lock before implementation.

The reason is simple: I deliberately removed the Google Fonts import in the previous version, so the browser is falling back to:

CSS

and because Space Grotesk isn't installed locally, you're getting the fallback.

Same problem with:

The serif is genuinely Georgia because we explicitly requested it.

About the dots

I can see the screenshot.

They're basically invisible.

We currently have:

CSS
opacity: 0.45;

but the actual dot:

CSS
rgba(255,255,255,0.14)

gets multiplied by that opacity.

So we're effectively ending up around:

0.14 × 0.45 ≈ 0.063

That's too fucking subtle on your monitor.

We'll change the approach.

Instead of:

CSS
.ecdat-grid {
    opacity: 0.45;
}

we'll use something like:

CSS
.ecdat-grid {
    opacity: 0.8;

    background-image:
        radial-gradient(
            circle,
            rgba(255,255,255,0.10) 1px,
            transparent 1.5px
        );
}

That gives us a visible but quiet field.

The dots should look like:

·        ·        ·        ·        ·

     ·        ·        ·        ·

·        ·        ·        ·        ·

not:

.....................................

and definitely not:

CYBER GRID OF DOOM 9000™

Your screenshot currently has a large amount of completely unused space above the hero because Streamlit itself is still giving us browser/app spacing.

For the final landing page, I want the hero to occupy the actual viewport, not feel like a Streamlit page containing a centered HTML element.

So Phase 1 isn't quite finished yet.

                BACKEND

That separation is gold.

4. BUT — I found several backend problems you need to know about

This is where we don't blindly trust our friend's code just because it runs.

Problem #1 — The scanner is regex-based

His scanner essentially does:

Python
Run
for line in lines:
    for algorithm, patterns in CRYPTO_PATTERNS.items():
        re.search(...)

That means it is pattern detection, not semantic code analysis.

For example:

Python
Run
.exchange(...)

is one of the ECDH patterns.

That's dangerously broad.

Something completely unrelated containing .exchange() could potentially be classified as ECDH.

So your UI should not say:

"ECDAT proves this application uses ECDH."

Better:

Detected artifact

and show the evidence.

That distinction matters.

5. Problem #2 — imports are intentionally ignored

His scanner says:

Python
Run
if stripped.startswith(("import ", "from ")):
    continue

That's actually sensible because:

Python
Run
from cryptography.hazmat.primitives.asymmetric import rsa

doesn't prove RSA is actually used.

But it also means the scanner isn't doing dependency analysis.

That's consistent with his roadmap.

So don't build a frontend screen claiming:

"Complete dependency cryptographic inventory."

It isn't.

6. Problem #3 — Mosca is not automatically calculated for the raw scanner output

This is important.

scan_repository() adds:

quantum_risk
risk_reason
recommendation

But Mosca fields are added later by build_dashboard_report().

So the pipeline is:

scan_repository()
       │
       ▼
raw-ish findings
       │
       ▼
build_dashboard_report()
       │
       ▼
Mosca fields added
       │
       ▼
final JSON

Your frontend should therefore consume the final dashboard report, not raw scanner findings.

That's exactly what:

output/scan_results.json

is.

7. Problem #4 — Current demo only scans ONE file

This is huge.

The current output says:

total_files_scanned: 1
total_artifacts: 6

because:

demo_repo/
└── payment_service.py

is literally one file.

So don't design the UI around the assumption that the backend is currently scanning a giant enterprise repository.

For the demo, we'll present:

DEMO REPOSITORY
1 file
6 detected artifacts

honestly.

When the scanner improves, the exact same UI can display:

ACME-PAYMENTS
14,382 files
427 artifacts

without redesigning the frontend.

8. Problem #5 — The current scanner has no business criticality

The Mosca calculation accepts:

Python
Run
business_criticality="Critical"

But that's manually supplied in:

Python
Run
export_scan.py

This:

Python
Run
mosca_inputs = {
    "data_lifetime": 12,
    "migration_time": 4,
    "quantum_horizon": 10,
    "business_criticality": "Critical",
}

is basically demo configuration.

It isn't discovered from the repository.

Therefore your UI should label this properly:

Assessment assumptions

rather than pretending ECDAT automatically determined them.

9. Problem #6 — The Mosca model is an assessment, not a prediction

The backend itself correctly says:

quantum_horizon is a planning assumption,
not a prediction.

Excellent.

Your frontend should preserve that.

For example:

QUANTUM HORIZON

10 years

Planning assumption
—not a predicted arrival date

That's much more credible.

10. Problem #7 — quantum_vulnerable_assets currently counts algorithms, not necessarily unique assets

The report does:

Python
Run
quantum_vulnerable_count = sum(
    1
    for artifact in artifacts
    if artifact["algorithm"]
    in QUANTUM_VULNERABLE_ALGORITHMS
)

So if the same underlying asset generates multiple artifacts, the count can potentially reflect detections rather than unique systems.

Again:

Frontend shouldn't reinterpret it.

Display:

Quantum-vulnerable artifacts

rather than:

Quantum-vulnerable systems

until the backend has stronger asset identity.

11. Problem #8 — File paths are Windows-style in the committed sample

The sample has:

demo_repo\payment_service.py

You're developing on Ubuntu.

Eventually we'd expect:

demo_repo/payment_service.py

Your frontend should normalize display paths:

demo_repo/payment_service.py

regardless of whether backend emits:

\

or:

/

That's a frontend normalization responsibility and a very easy win.

12. Problem #9 — Evidence is truncated

The scanner does:

Python
Run
"evidence": stripped[:300]

That's okay for the UI.

But we should build the artifact evidence component so it handles:

short evidence
long evidence
missing evidence
multiple lines

without assuming evidence always exists.

13. Problem #10 — The backend currently doesn't have an actual frontend

I don't see a dashboard implementation in the ZIP.

So your current work isn't conflicting with his UI.

Excellent.

We can build the frontend cleanly.

14. Here's the architecture I now recommend

Forget the structure I gave you earlier slightly.

After seeing his actual repository, this is better:

ECDAT/
│
├── app.py                         ← YOUR FRONTEND ENTRY
│
├── ui/                            ← YOUR UI
│   ├── __init__.py
│   ├── theme.py

│   ├── components.py

│   ├── tables.py
│   ├── badges.py
│   └── charts.py
│
├── pages_ui/                      ← YOUR PAGES
│   ├── overview.py
│   ├── inventory.py
│   ├── artifact.py
│   ├── quantum.py
│   ├── mosca.py
│   ├── migration.py
│   ├── import_scan.py
│   └── reports.py
│
├── frontend/
│   ├── __init__.py
│   ├── data_loader.py
│   ├── normalizer.py
│   └── state.py
│
├── assets/
│   └── fonts/
│

├── analysis/                      ← FRIEND
├── demo_repo/                     ← FRIEND

├── output/                        ← FRIEND
│
├── export_scan.py                 ← FRIEND
├── test_scan.py                   ← FRIEND
├── test_mosca.py                  ← FRIEND
│
└── requirements.txt

This makes ownership painfully obvious.

Your territory
ui/
pages_ui/
frontend/
assets/
app.py
His territory
scanner/
analysis/
export_scan.py

That's how two people avoid stepping on each other's fucking toes.

15. And here's the really important part

We don't need to wait for him.

We'll build against:

examples/sample_scan_results.json

Initially.

Then switch the source to:

output/scan_results.json

when running a real scan.

Eventually:

Backend function
       ↓
Python object
       ↓
Frontend adapter

The UI doesn't care.

16. Our frontend now has a concrete data contract

Based on his actual current report, our adapter should expose:

Python
Run
ScanViewModel(
    target,
    generated_at,
    scope,
    files_scanned,
    artifacts,
    quantum_vulnerable_assets,
    risk_summary,
    mosca_inputs
)

and each artifact:

Python
Run
ArtifactViewModel(
    algorithm,
    type,
    key_size,
    mode,
    curve,
    version,
    file,
    line,
    evidence,
    quantum_risk,
    risk_reason,
    mosca_risk,
    mosca_status,
    mosca_explanation,
    recommendation
)

This is the layer that protects your frontend from backend changes.

18. One thing I want us to change from the earlier plan

We were about to build the application shell immediately.

Don't.

Now that I've inspected his repository, the smarter next step is:

Phase 2A — Frontend Data Adapter

Before making the dashboard beautiful, we create:

frontend/data_loader.py
frontend/normalizer.py

Why?

Because then your UI never directly touches:

Python
Run
results["artifacts"][3]["details"]["key_size"]

That is ugly, brittle frontend code.

Instead:

Python
Run
artifact.key_size

Much cleaner.

And if your friend changes:

details.key_size

to:

key_size

you change one adapter, not the entire dashboard.

That's maintainability.

19. So our revised build order is
PHASE 1
Visual identity
        ✓ DONE

PHASE 2A
Data adapter
        ← NEXT

PHASE 2B
Application shell

PHASE 3
Overview

PHASE 4
Inventory / CBOM

PHASE 5
Artifact investigation

PHASE 6
Quantum risk

PHASE 7
Mosca assessment

PHASE 8
Migration recommendations

PHASE 9
Import / reports

PHASE 10
Security hardening

PHASE 11
Integration with friend's final backend

PHASE 12
Demo polish

And we don't modify his backend to make our frontend easier.

That's the rule.

Bottom line

Your friend isn't bullshitting about having a working 20%-ish prototype. There is a real functioning discovery → analysis → report pipeline. But it is still a prototype, with regex-based discovery, a single demo file, manually supplied Mosca assumptions, and several enterprise capabilities explicitly not implemented yet.

That means your frontend should present the current evidence honestly while being architected to grow when his backend grows.

And now we know exactly what we're building against.

Next step: Phase 2A — I'll give you the complete frontend/data_loader.py + frontend/normalizer.py, test them against his existing output/scan_results.json, and we won't touch a single backend file.

Yes. This is the right time to stop coding for a minute and choose the visual direction. I searched current 2026 dashboard/product patterns and security UI examples rather than giving you the same generic “sidebar + four cards + chart” garbage.

The current direction is interesting: modern product UIs are getting denser but calmer. shadcn's May 2026 Rhea style explicitly moved toward tighter spacing and denser surfaces for focused product interfaces, while its current dashboard blocks emphasize composable sidebars, tables, charts, and structured navigation. 
shadcn/ui
+1
 Vercel's interface guidelines also emphasize stable layouts, clear hierarchy, designed empty/error/dense states, tabular numbers, and redundant status cues rather than relying on color alone. 
Vercel

For cybersecurity specifically, current concepts are still heavily dark/data-dense, but the better ones are moving toward clear prioritization instead of maximum neon. 
Behance
+1

And Wiz's current product positioning is a useful conceptual reference for us: show relationships/context and prioritize the next action, rather than dumping raw security data onto the user. 
wiz.io
+1

Correct. The current dots are too subtle. We overcorrected.

The requirement wasn't "make dots technically present." It was a visible dot-grid atmosphere that remains subordinate to the hero. A good dotted grid needs enough spacing, radius, and contrast that the pattern can be perceived without becoming the content. Current implementations commonly expose exactly those controls—spacing, dot size/radius, and contrast—and accessibility guidance recommends keeping decorative grids behind the foreground and respecting reduced motion. 
Originkit
+2
Hyperiux Vault
+2

What I'd change

Current:

CSS
rgba(255, 255, 255, 0.10)
0.7px
24px spacing
opacity: 0.45

That's effectively:

"Congratulations, there are dots. Find them if you have a microscope."

Instead use:

dot size:        ~1.3px
spacing:         28px
dot opacity:     ~0.16
field opacity:   0.75

And don't make the dots brighter everywhere. We'll give the field a subtle spatial variation so sections of the page naturally reveal the grid.

Replace only the dot-field CSS

In:

Bash
cd ~/ECDAT/ECDAT1/frontend
nano app/globals.css

Find:

CSS
.ecdat-dot-field {

Replace that entire .ecdat-dot-field block with:

CSS
.ecdat-dot-field {
  position: fixed;
  inset: 0;
  z-index: 0;

  pointer-events: none;

  background-image: radial-gradient(
    circle,
    rgba(255, 255, 255, 0.17) 0,
    rgba(255, 255, 255, 0.17) 1.1px,
    transparent 1.2px
  );

  background-size: 28px 28px;

  opacity: 0.78;

  animation: ecdat-drift 12s ease-in-out infinite alternate;
}

Then change the animation to:

CSS
@keyframes ecdat-drift {
  0% {
    transform: translate3d(-8px, -4px, 0);
  }

  50% {
    transform: translate3d(6px, 8px, 0);
  }

  100% {
    transform: translate3d(12px, -6px, 0);
  }
}
But I want one more improvement

The dots shouldn't have the same visual strength across the entire screen.

That's what makes a dot background look like a CSS demo.

We're going to add a second layer that subtly masks the grid around the hero.

Add this after .ecdat-dot-field:

CSS
.ecdat-dot-field::after {
  content: "";
  position: absolute;
  inset: 0;

  background:
    radial-gradient(
      ellipse 65% 55% at 50% 48%,
      rgba(14, 21, 34, 0.88) 0%,
      rgba(14, 21, 34, 0.62) 32%,
      rgba(14, 21, 34, 0.18) 65%,
      transparent 100%
    );

  pointer-events: none;
}

Now you get:

MORE VISIBLE
·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·

    ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·
       ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·
          ┌─────────────┐
          │             │
          │    ECDAT    │
          │             │
          └─────────────┘
       ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·
    ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·

·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·   ·
MORE VISIBLE

So the periphery reveals the grid, while the central typography gets breathing room.

And yes, your backend ZIP stays untouched. We're still only building the frontend.

Right now, we've already used the ZIP. You don't need to wait for some mythical "final integration phase."

You extracted the backend into:

~/ECDAT/ECDAT1/backend/

and your current frontend is:

~/ECDAT/ECDAT1/frontend/

So the relationship is currently:

ECDAT1/
│
├── backend/          ← your friend's code
│   ├── analysis/
│   ├── scanner/
│   ├── demo_repo/
│   ├── examples/
│   ├── output/
│   │   └── scan_results.json   ← REAL BACKEND OUTPUT
│   ├── export_scan.py
│   └── README.md
│
└── frontend/         ← your work
    ├── app/
    ├── components/
    ├── lib/
    └── ...

And the backend's own README explicitly defines the intended pipeline as:

Source Repository → Discovery → Normalization → Classification → Quantum Risk → Mosca Assessment → PQC Recommendation → Dashboard-ready JSON. 

Pasted markdown

So here's the important distinction

We are not supposed to merge the backend into the Next.js frontend.

We're going to make the frontend consume the backend's output.

For development right now:

backend/output/scan_results.json
                ↓
        frontend adapter
                ↓
          Overview UI
                ↓
       Inventory UI
                ↓
       Quantum Risk UI
                ↓
        MOSCA UI
                ↓
       Migration UI
                ↓
         Reports UI

That's actually cleaner than throwing the Python backend into frontend/ and creating a motherfucker of a monorepo with everything tangled together.

Next.js App Router supports server-side data access directly from Server Components, and its documentation specifically recommends accessing the data source directly rather than making a Server Component call its own Route Handler. 
Next.js
+1

When do we use the backend ZIP?

Now.

We already staged it. The next frontend milestone is:

Phase 1 — UI shell

✅ Next.js created
✅ Fonts
✅ Landing page
✅ Dot atmosphere
✅ Sidebar
✅ Topbar
✅ Overview route

Phase 2 — Real backend data

We are here.

We'll take this:

backend/output/scan_results.json

and build:

frontend/lib/scan-data.ts

That adapter will turn your friend's raw backend structure into a clean frontend model.

For example:

backend JSON
     │
     ├── total_files_scanned
     ├── total_artifacts
     ├── quantum_vulnerable_assets
     ├── risk_summary
     ├── mosca_inputs
     └── artifacts[]
                │
                ▼
       scan-data.ts
                │
                ▼
        typed frontend data

Your actual JSON already gives us real values, including:

Files scanned:             1
Artifacts:                 6
Quantum vulnerable:        2

Medium:                    1

And the six actual artifacts include:

TLS       MEDIUM

So we can stop displaying the fake:

18
6

and make the dashboard actually reflect your friend's scanner.

And later, the ZIP's Python code becomes important

There are two stages.

Stage A — read the generated result

We're doing this now.

Frontend reads:

backend/output/scan_results.json

This lets us build the entire UI against real ECDAT data.

Stage B — live scanning

Later we'll connect the frontend to the actual Python scanner:

frontend
   │
   │ "Run Scan"
   ▼
backend
   │
   ├── scanner/crypto_scanner.py
   ├── analysis/quantum_risk.py
   ├── analysis/mosca.py
   ├── analysis/recommendations.py
   └── analysis/report_builder.py
   │
   ▼
scan results
   │
   ▼
frontend

That's when buttons such as:

Run Scan

Import Repository

Refresh Analysis

Export Report

actually trigger backend functionality.

We're not there yet, because there's no point wiring interactive controls to an API before the UI/data contract is settled. That's how projects end up with 47 endpoints and three different definitions of "risk." Beautiful disaster.

So our immediate next move

Don't touch the ZIP.

Don't extract anything again.

Don't modify backend/.

Don't create an API yet.

We're going to create:

Bash
cd ~/ECDAT/ECDAT1/frontend
nano lib/scan-data.ts

and make that adapter read:

../backend/output/scan_results.json

Then we'll rebuild /overview using the actual six artifacts and risk distribution from your friend's backend.

That is the correct boundary:

Your friend owns the analysis. You own how humans understand the analysis.

And the backend README explicitly says the structured representation is intended to remain independent from dashboard/reporting components, which is exactly the separation we're going to preserve.

Yes. The sidebar typography is good overall, but I’d make two surgical changes before we lock it.

From the screenshots:

Element	Current feel	Verdict
ECDAT logo	~16px, bold	✅ Good
Sidebar section labels	~8–9px mono	✅ Exactly right
Navigation items	~13px	✅ Good
Sidebar secondary text	~9px mono	✅ Good
Version/footer	~8–9px	⚠️ Slightly too small
Active item	~13px + medium weight	✅ Good
The important part: don't make the nav bigger

Your sidebar is already behaving like an enterprise/security tool, not a generic SaaS dashboard.

The hierarchy is clean:

ECDAT                    ← Space Grotesk / ~16px / 600

CRYPTOGRAPHIC ANALYSIS   ← JetBrains Mono / ~8px

DISCOVER                 ← JetBrains Mono / ~8px
  Overview               ← Space Grotesk / ~13px
  Inventory              ← Space Grotesk / ~13px

ASSESS                   ← JetBrains Mono / ~8px
  Quantum Risk           ← Space Grotesk / ~13px
  Mosca Analysis         ← Space Grotesk / ~13px

PLAN
  Migration

REPORT
  Reports

That's actually a strong typographic system. Don't let some Tailwind-size-maxxing motherfucker convince you that every label needs to be 16px.

One thing I'd change

The sidebar's inactive navigation text is a little too dim.

Not too small — too low-contrast.

The screenshot shows:

Inventory
Quantum Risk
Mosca Analysis

sitting fairly quietly against the navy. That's aesthetically nice, but an enterprise application needs those items to remain immediately scannable.

I'd use approximately:

CSS
--sidebar-text: #8793A6;
--sidebar-text-hover: #C5CEDB;
--sidebar-active: #F4F6F8;

And keep the section labels subdued.

font-size: 13px;
font-weight: 500;

Section labels:

CSS
font-family: var(--font-mono);
font-size: 9px;
font-weight: 500;
letter-spacing: 0.18em;
text-transform: uppercase;

Logo:

CSS
font-family: var(--font-space);
font-size: 16px;
font-weight: 600;

Data / counts:

CSS
font-family: var(--font-mono);

That's the hierarchy I'd lock.

function RiskBadge({ risk }: { risk: string | null }) {
  if (!risk) {
    return <span className="text-xs text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">—</span>;
  }

  return (
    <span className={`risk-badge risk-${risk.toLowerCase()}`}>
      {risk}
    </span>
  );
}

export default function ArtifactsPage() {
  const [selected, setSelected] = useState(0);

  const artifact = artifacts[selected];

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-[1220px] px-10 py-10">
      <div className="mb-8">
        <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.22em] text-[var(--primary)]">
          Discovery / Artifacts
        </p>

        <h1 className="mt-3 text-3xl font-semibold tracking-tight">
          Cryptographic artifacts
        </h1>

        <p className="mt-3 max-w-3xl text-sm leading-6 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
          Inspect individual cryptographic findings, source evidence,
          risk assessments, and recommended migration direction.
        </p>
      </div>

      <div className="grid grid-cols-[360px_1fr] gap-5">
        {/* Artifact list */}
        <section className="overflow-hidden rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)]">
          <div className="border-b border-[var(--border)] px-5 py-4">
            <div className="flex items-center justify-between">
              <span className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
                Findings
              </span>

              <span className="font-mono text-xs text-[var(--primary)]">
                {artifacts.length}
              </span>
            </div>
          </div>

          {artifacts.map((item, index) => (
            <button
              key={`${item.algorithm}-${item.line}`}
              onClick={() => setSelected(index)}
              className={`block w-full border-b border-[var(--border)] px-5 py-4 text-left transition-colors last:border-b-0 ${
                selected === index
                  ? "bg-[var(--accent)]"
                  : "hover:bg-[var(--accent)]"
              }`}
            >
              <div className="flex items-center justify-between gap-4">
                <span className="font-mono text-sm font-medium">
                  {item.algorithm}
                </span>

                <RiskBadge risk={item.quantumRisk} />
              </div>

              <div className="mt-2 font-mono text-[10px] text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
                {item.file}
              </div>

              <div className="mt-1 font-mono text-[10px] text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
                LINE {item.line}
              </div>
            </button>
          ))}
        </section>

        {/* Detail */}
        <section className="space-y-5">
          <div className="rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)]">
            <div className="flex items-start justify-between border-b border-[var(--border)] px-6 py-5">
              <div>
                <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
                  Artifact identity
                </p>

                <h2 className="mt-2 text-2xl font-semibold">
                  {artifact.algorithm}
                </h2>
              </div>

              <RiskBadge risk={artifact.quantumRisk} />
            </div>

            <div className="grid grid-cols-4 gap-px bg-[var(--border)]">
              <Info label="Type" value={artifact.type} />
              <Info
                label="Key size"
                value={artifact.keySize ? `${artifact.keySize} bit` : "—"}
              />
              <Info label="Mode" value={artifact.mode ?? "—"} />
              <Info label="Curve" value={artifact.curve ?? "—"} />
            </div>
          </div>

          <div className="rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)]">
            <div className="border-b border-[var(--border)] px-6 py-5">
              <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--primary)]">
                Discovery evidence
              </p>
            </div>

            <div className="px-6 py-5">
              <div className="grid grid-cols-[90px_1fr] gap-y-3">
                <span className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
                  File
                </span>

                <span className="font-mono text-sm">
                  {artifact.file}
                </span>

                <span className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
                  Line
                </span>

                <span className="font-mono text-sm">
                  {artifact.line}
                </span>
              </div>

              <div className="mt-5 rounded-md border border-[var(--border)] bg-[#0a101a] px-4 py-4">
                <code className="font-mono text-xs text-[var(--foreground)]">
                  {artifact.evidence}
                </code>
              </div>
            </div>
          </div>

          <div className="grid grid-cols-2 gap-5">
            <div className="rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)] p-6">
              <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--primary)]">
                Quantum assessment
              </p>

              <div className="mt-4 flex items-center justify-between">
                <h3 className="text-lg font-medium">
                  Quantum risk
                </h3>

                <RiskBadge risk={artifact.quantumRisk} />
              </div>

              <p className="mt-4 text-sm leading-6 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
                {artifact.riskReason}
              </p>
            </div>

            <div className="rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)] p-6">
              <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--primary)]">
                Mosca assessment
              </p>

              <div className="mt-4 flex items-center justify-between">
                <h3 className="text-lg font-medium">
                  Migration risk
                </h3>

                <RiskBadge risk={artifact.moscaRisk} />
              </div>

              <p className="mt-4 text-sm leading-6 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
                {artifact.moscaExplanation ??
                  "No Mosca assessment is currently available for this artifact."}
              </p>
            </div>
          </div>

          <div className="rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)] p-6">
            <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--primary)]">
              Recommended migration
            </p>

            <h3 className="mt-3 text-lg font-medium">
              Recommended direction
            </h3>

            <p className="mt-3 max-w-4xl text-sm leading-6 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
              {artifact.recommendation}
            </p>

            <div className="mt-5 border-t border-[var(--border)] pt-4">
              <p className="text-xs leading-5 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
                Final algorithm selection depends on the cryptographic
                purpose, implementation requirements, organizational
                policy, and applicable standards.
              </p>
            </div>
          </div>
        </section>
      </div>
    </main>
  );
}

function Info({
  label,
  value,
}: {
  label: string;
  value: string;
}) {
  return (
    <div className="bg-[var(--card)] px-5 py-4">
      <p className="font-mono text-[9px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
        {label}
      </p>

      <p className="mt-2 font-mono text-sm">
        {value}
      </p>
    </div>
  );
}

Important: I'm deliberately using the backend's actual artifact fields here rather than inventing a different frontend data model. The project specification explicitly says the dashboard should depend on the JSON/data contract, not scanner internals, so when we eventually connect backend/output/scan_results.json, this page should need only a data-loader swap. 

ECDAT — Enterprise Cryptographi…

Build this first. Then send me the screenshot of /artifacts. We'll judge spacing and hierarchy before touching Quantum Risk.

Sources

Yep. Now this actually looks like an application, not a CSS crime scene.

The Inventory page is locked enough to move on.

What I see

Typography: good. Space Grotesk carries the UI, JetBrains Mono handles technical labels/data. Keep it.

Hierarchy: good. Page title → description → filter bar → table is clean.

Density: appropriate for an enterprise security tool. Don't turn this into giant-card SaaS kindergarten.

Risk badges: correctly separated from the ECDAT yellow.

Table: readable and restrained.

Sidebar: consistent with Overview.

Yellow: restrained enough that it still means action/identity, rather than screaming at the user like some amateur dashboard.

One tiny thing I'd change later: the table has a lot of empty horizontal space between columns. That's not broken, but when we add real asset/file information, we'll use that space rather than artificially tightening it.

So, next move

Don't connect the backend yet.

Build the remaining screens in this order:

/overview       ✅
/inventory      ✅

/artifacts      ← next
/quantum
/mosca
/migration
/reports
/import

Next we'll build /artifacts, because that's where the inventory starts becoming useful: individual cryptographic findings, source file, line number, algorithm, risk, evidence, etc.

Then we'll connect the backend once the UI has somewhere sensible to put its output.

That avoids the classic motherfucker mistake of wiring APIs into half-designed pages and then redesigning the entire frontend around whatever JSON happened to come out.

Yep. Next: Migration. This is the screen where ECDAT stops being a pretty scanner and starts answering the question: “What the hell do we do about these findings?”

The source spec says migration must distinguish public-key key establishment, digital signatures, symmetric crypto, and legacy/insecure algorithms rather than blindly assigning PQC algorithms. 

ECDAT — Enterprise Cryptographi…

Your backend already gives us exactly the recommendations we need, including RSA → ML-KEM/hybrid or ML-DSA/SLH-DSA depending on purpose, ECDSA → ML-DSA/SLH-DSA, AES-256 as lower priority, and MD5 as immediate replacement.

TypeScript
"use client";

const priorities = [
  {
    priority: "01",
    algorithm: "MD5",
    category: "LEGACY / INSECURE",
    urgency: "CRITICAL",
    action: "Replace immediately",
    direction:
      "Replace MD5 with an approved modern hash construction appropriate to the specific security use case.",
    reason:
      "MD5 is already cryptographically broken independently of future quantum-computing threats.",
  },
  {
    priority: "02",
    algorithm: "RSA",
    category: "PUBLIC-KEY",
    urgency: "HIGH",
    action: "Begin migration planning",
    direction:
      "For key establishment or encryption workflows, evaluate ML-KEM or an appropriate hybrid mechanism. For digital signatures, evaluate ML-DSA, SLH-DSA, or an appropriate hybrid signature.",
    reason:
      "RSA is vulnerable to sufficiently capable cryptographically relevant quantum computers using Shor's algorithm.",
  },
  {
    priority: "03",
    algorithm: "ECDSA",
    category: "DIGITAL SIGNATURE",
    urgency: "HIGH",
    action: "Begin migration planning",
    direction:
      "Evaluate migration toward ML-DSA, SLH-DSA, or an appropriate hybrid digital-signature mechanism.",
    reason:
      "Elliptic-curve public-key cryptography is vulnerable to sufficiently capable quantum attacks.",
  },
  {
    priority: "04",
    algorithm: "TLS",
    category: "PROTOCOL",
    urgency: "MEDIUM",
    action: "Inspect configuration",
    direction:
      "Inspect the negotiated cipher suites, authentication algorithm and key-establishment mechanism. Plan approved PQC or hybrid TLS mechanisms where required.",
    reason:
      "TLS quantum exposure depends on the algorithms configured inside the protocol.",
  },
  {
    priority: "05",
    algorithm: "AES",
    category: "SYMMETRIC",
    urgency: "LOW",
    action: "Continue monitoring",
    direction:
      "No urgent PQC replacement is required when using an appropriate key size such as AES-256.",
    reason:
      "AES-256 retains substantial security against known quantum attack models.",
  },
  {
    priority: "06",
    algorithm: "SHA-256",
    category: "HASH",
    urgency: "LOW",
    action: "Continue monitoring",
    direction:
      "No immediate post-quantum migration is required. Continue using an approved modern hash construction appropriate to the application's security requirements.",
    reason:
      "SHA-256 is affected less dramatically by known quantum algorithms than RSA or elliptic-curve cryptography.",
  },
];

export default function MigrationPage() {
  const critical = priorities.filter((item) => item.urgency === "CRITICAL");
  const high = priorities.filter((item) => item.urgency === "HIGH");
  const medium = priorities.filter((item) => item.urgency === "MEDIUM");
  const low = priorities.filter((item) => item.urgency === "LOW");

  return (
    <main className="mx-auto max-w-[1220px] px-10 py-10">
      {/* HEADER */}
      <header className="mb-9 flex items-start justify-between">
        <div>
          <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.22em] text-[var(--primary)]">
            Plan / Migration
          </p>

          <h1 className="mt-3 text-3xl font-semibold tracking-tight">
            Migration planning
          </h1>

          <p className="mt-3 max-w-3xl text-sm leading-6 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
            Prioritize cryptographic findings and map each asset toward an
            appropriate remediation or post-quantum migration direction.
          </p>
        </div>

        <div className="text-right">
          <p className="font-mono text-[9px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
            Migration queue
          </p>

          <p className="mt-2 font-mono text-lg">
            {priorities.length}
            <span className="ml-2 text-xs text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
              findings
            </span>
          </p>
        </div>
      </header>

      {/* SUMMARY */}
      <section className="mb-5 grid grid-cols-4 gap-4">
        <SummaryCard
          label="Critical"
          value={critical.length}
          description="Immediate remediation"
          tone="danger"
        />

        <SummaryCard
          label="High"
          value={high.length}
          description="Migration planning"
          tone="warning"
        />

        <SummaryCard
          label="Medium"
          value={medium.length}
          description="Configuration review"
          tone="warning"
        />

        <SummaryCard
          label="Low"
          value={low.length}
          description="Monitor"
          tone="success"
        />
      </section>

      {/* STRATEGY */}
      <section className="mb-5 rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)]">
        <div className="border-b border-[var(--border)] px-6 py-5">
          <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--primary)]">
            Migration strategy
          </p>

          <h2 className="mt-2 text-lg font-medium">
            Remediation follows cryptographic purpose
          </h2>
        </div>

        <div className="grid grid-cols-4 gap-px bg-[var(--border)]">
          <Strategy
            title="Key establishment"
            algorithms="RSA · ECDH · DH"
            direction="Evaluate ML-KEM and appropriate hybrid mechanisms."
          />

          <Strategy
            title="Digital signatures"
            algorithms="RSA · ECDSA"
            direction="Evaluate ML-DSA, SLH-DSA, or appropriate hybrid signatures."
          />

          <Strategy
            title="Symmetric encryption"
            algorithms="AES-256"
            direction="Lower PQC migration urgency. Maintain appropriate key sizes."
          />

          <Strategy
            title="Legacy cryptography"
            algorithms="MD5 · SHA-1 · DES"
            direction="Remediate independently of future quantum threats."
          />
        </div>
      </section>

      {/* QUEUE */}
      <section className="rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)]">
        <div className="flex items-center justify-between border-b border-[var(--border)] px-6 py-5">
          <div>
            <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--primary)]">
              Priority findings
            </p>

            <h2 className="mt-2 text-lg font-medium">
              Recommended migration queue
            </h2>
          </div>

          <span className="font-mono text-[9px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
            ordered by urgency
          </span>
        </div>

        <div>
          {priorities.map((item) => (
            <MigrationRow key={item.algorithm} item={item} />
          ))}
        </div>
      </section>

      {/* IMPORTANT DISTINCTION */}
      <section className="mt-5 grid grid-cols-2 gap-5">
        <div className="rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)] p-6">
          <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--danger)]">
            Already insecure
          </p>

          <h2 className="mt-3 text-lg font-medium">
            Legacy cryptography
          </h2>

          <p className="mt-3 text-sm leading-6 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
            Algorithms such as MD5 require remediation because they are already
            cryptographically insecure. This is separate from the future
            quantum-threat migration problem.
          </p>
        </div>

        <div className="rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)] p-6">
          <p className="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--primary)]">
            Future exposure
          </p>

          <h2 className="mt-3 text-lg font-medium">
            Post-quantum migration
          </h2>

          <p className="mt-3 text-sm leading-6 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
            RSA and elliptic-curve public-key cryptography require migration
            planning because sufficiently capable cryptographically relevant
            quantum computers could threaten their underlying mathematical
            assumptions.
          </p>
        </div>
      </section>

      {/* DISCLAIMER */}
      <section className="mt-5 border-l-2 border-[var(--primary)] bg-[var(--card)] px-6 py-5">
        <p className="font-mono text-[9px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-[var(--primary)]">
          Decision support
        </p>

        <p className="mt-2 text-sm leading-6 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
          Migration directions are recommendations for planning. The final
          algorithm choice depends on cryptographic purpose, application
          requirements, interoperability, performance, and organizational
          standards.
        </p>
      </section>
    </main>
  );
}

function SummaryCard({
  label,
  value,
  description,
  tone,
}: {
  label: string;
  value: number;
  description: string;
  tone: "danger" | "warning" | "success";
}) {
  const toneClass =
    tone === "danger"
      ? "text-[var(--danger)]"
      : tone === "success"
        ? "text-[var(--success)]"
        : "text-[var(--warning)]";

  return (
    <div className="rounded-lg border border-[var(--border)] bg-[var(--card)] p-5">
      <p className="font-mono text-[9px] uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
        {label}
      </p>

      <p className={`mt-3 font-mono text-3xl ${toneClass}`}>
        {value}
      </p>

      <p className="mt-2 text-xs text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
        {description}
      </p>
    </div>
  );
}

function Strategy({
  title,
  algorithms,
  direction,
}: {
  title: string;
  algorithms: string;
  direction: string;
}) {
  return (
    <div className="bg-[var(--card)] p-5">
      <p className="text-sm font-medium">{title}</p>

      <p className="mt-2 font-mono text-[10px] text-[var(--primary)]">
        {algorithms}
      </p>

      <p className="mt-3 text-xs leading-5 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
        {direction}
      </p>
    </div>
  );
}

function MigrationRow({
  item,
}: {
  item: (typeof priorities)[number];
}) {
  const urgencyClass =
    item.urgency === "CRITICAL"
      ? "risk-critical"
      : item.urgency === "HIGH"
        ? "risk-high"
        : item.urgency === "MEDIUM"
          ? "risk-medium"
          : "risk-low";

  return (
    <article className="border-b border-[var(--border)] px-6 py-6 last:border-b-0">
      <div className="grid grid-cols-[60px_150px_1fr_150px] gap-5">
        <div className="font-mono text-sm text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
          {item.priority}
        </div>

        <div>
          <div className="font-mono text-sm">{item.algorithm}</div>

          <p className="mt-2 font-mono text-[8px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
            {item.category}
          </p>
        </div>

        <div>
          <p className="text-sm font-medium">{item.action}</p>

          <p className="mt-2 text-xs leading-5 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
            {item.direction}
          </p>

          <p className="mt-3 border-l border-[var(--border)] pl-3 text-xs leading-5 text-[var(--muted-foreground)]">
            {item.reason}
          </p>
        </div>

        <div className="flex justify-end">
          <span className={`risk-badge ${urgencyClass}`}>
            {item.urgency}
          </span>
        </div>
      </div>
    </article>
  );
}

Step 1 — Create the API server
