    artifact_id = artifact["artifact_id"]

and later:

build_verification_state()
    verification_id = stable_id("verification", artifact["artifact_id"])

So the correct boundary is now obvious:

SCANNER
  ↓
raw finding / observation
  ↓
CANONICAL IDENTITY LAYER
  ├── artifact_id       ← semantic
  ├── evidence_id       ← observation
  ├── risk_id
  ├── recommendation_id
  ├── migration_id
  └── verification_id

The canonical layer should decide:

"This observation belongs to cryptographic artifact X."

That's much more robust.

canonical artifact

everything else derives IDs from that

We're changing it to:

scanner finding
  ↓
canonical identity
  ├── semantic artifact_id
  └── observation evidence_id
       ↓
risk / MOSCA / recommendation / migration / verification
       ↓
all reference the semantic artifact

The scanner can continue emitting its old artifact_id temporarily. The canonical layer will stop trusting it.

The important part is that we need deterministic occurrence handling. Rather than introducing an unstable occurrence=0,1,2 based on scanner traversal order, we'll use the scanner's existing finding characteristics to establish a deterministic semantic key.

def build_evidence(
    artifact: Dict[str, Any],
    artifact_id: str,
) -> Evidence:
    """
    Convert one scanner observation into canonical evidence.

    Evidence identity intentionally retains source location because
    evidence represents where and how the artifact was observed.
    """
    evidence_id = build_evidence_id(
        artifact_id=artifact_id,
        file=str(artifact.get("file", "")),
        line=int(artifact.get("line", 0)),
        evidence=str(artifact.get("evidence", "")),
    )

    context = artifact.get("evidence_context") or []

    return Evidence(
        evidence_id=evidence_id,
        file=str(artifact.get("file", "")),
        line=int(artifact.get("line", 0)),
        text=str(artifact.get("evidence", "")),
        context=context,
    )
Third change: create canonical artifact identity

Add this function immediately before build_canonical_artifact():

Python
Run
def build_canonical_artifact_id(
    artifact: Dict[str, Any],
    application_id: str,
) -> str:
    """
    Build stable logical identity for a cryptographic artifact.

    Source file and line number are deliberately excluded.

    The scanner's artifact_id is an observation identifier and must
    not become the canonical enterprise identity.
    """
    algorithm = str(
        artifact.get("algorithm", "UNKNOWN")
    )

    artifact_type = str(
        artifact.get(
            "type",
            "cryptographic_artifact",
        )
    )

    purpose = str(
        artifact.get(
            "purpose",
            "unknown",
        )
    )

    details = dict(
        artifact.get("details", {})
    )

    # Preserve meaningful semantic attributes that may be emitted
    # outside the nested details object.
    for key in (
        "key_size",
        "mode",
        "curve",
        "version",
        "algorithm_family",
    ):
        value = artifact.get(key)

        if value is not None:
            details[key] = value

    return build_artifact_id(
        application_id=application_id,
        algorithm=algorithm,
        artifact_type=artifact_type,
        purpose=purpose,
        details=details,
    )
BUT — one problem

This first semantic ID would collapse two genuinely distinct RSA findings that have exactly the same semantic properties.

We need to solve that inside the canonical scan construction, where we can see the entire set of findings.

So we're going to introduce a deterministic occurrence discriminator there.

Python

occurrence = artifact_occurrences.get(semantic_key, 0)

And here's the identity design we're implementing

We're going to introduce a stable semantic source signature.

Conceptually:

scanner finding
│
├── algorithm
├── type
├── purpose
├── crypto details
├── detection method
├── normalized source expression
└── semantic context
        │
        ▼
semantic_signature
        │
        ▼
canonical artifact ID

Not:

line 10 → artifact A
line 20 → artifact B

Instead:

RSA.generate_private_key(A)
        ↓
artifact X

RSA.generate_private_key(B)
        ↓
artifact Y

And if the code moves:

line 10 → line 80

the semantic signature stays the same.

One crucial rule

If two findings are genuinely indistinguishable from the available source evidence:

RSA.generate_private_key()
RSA.generate_private_key()

with no distinct variable/function/symbol/context we can reliably extract, ECDAT will not manufacture fake identities.

They can become:

artifact X
   ├── evidence #1
   └── evidence #2

That's actually the correct model.

Later, when we make the scanner more sophisticated—AST/symbol/function/class extraction, key fingerprints, configuration scopes, binary/library identities, etc.—we can split those observations when the evidence supports it.

That's how we keep ECDAT evidence-driven rather than bullshit-driven.

Send the remaining canonical.py output and we'll make the identity layer properly.

cat > backend/model/scan_state_builder.py <<'PY'
from typing import Any, Dict

from model.identity import build_scan_id
from model.scan_state import ScanState


def build_scan_state(report: Dict[str, Any]) -> ScanState:
    """
    Build a historical ECDAT scan snapshot from the canonical report.

    The snapshot intentionally preserves the analytical source data
    required for future diffing without rescanning the repository.

    Scan identity is derived from the logical application identity
    and scan generation timestamp. Source paths are not embedded in
    the public scan identifier.
    """

    metadata = report.get("metadata", {}) or {}

    applications = report.get("applications", []) or []
    canonical_artifacts = (
        report.get("canonical_artifacts", []) or []
    )
    evidence = report.get("evidence", []) or []
    business_contexts = (
        report.get("business_contexts", []) or []
    )
    relationships = (
        report.get("relationships", []) or []
    )

    application_ids = [
        str(application.get("application_id"))
        for application in applications
        if application.get("application_id")
    ]

    artifact_ids = [
        str(artifact.get("artifact_id"))
        for artifact in canonical_artifacts
        if artifact.get("artifact_id")
    ]

    generated_at = metadata.get("generated_at")
    target = metadata.get("target")

    if not generated_at:
        generated_at = "unknown"

    if application_ids:
        scan_application_id = application_ids[0]
    else:
        scan_application_id = "application:unknown"

    scan_id = build_scan_id(
        application_id=scan_application_id,
        generated_at=str(generated_at),
    )

    return ScanState(
        scan_id=scan_id,
        application_ids=application_ids,
        generated_at=generated_at,
        target=target,
        artifact_ids=artifact_ids,
        canonical_artifacts=canonical_artifacts,
        evidence=evidence,
        business_contexts=business_contexts,
        relationships=relationships,
        risk_landscape=(
            report.get("risk_landscape", {}) or {}
        ),
        summary=(
            report.get("summary", {}) or {}
        ),
        metadata=metadata,
    )
PY

Bash
python -m compileall backend

from model.scan_state_builder import build_scan_state

report = {
    "metadata": {
        "target": "/home/example/enterprise/payment-service",
        "generated_at": "2026-09-05T12:00:00+00:00",
    },
    "applications": [
        {
            "application_id": "application:payment-service",
        }
    ],
    "canonical_artifacts": [],
    "evidence": [],
    "business_contexts": [],
    "relationships": [],
    "summary": {},
}

state = build_scan_state(report)

print("Scan ID :", state.scan_id)
print("Target  :", state.target)

assert state.scan_id.startswith("scan:")
assert "/home/" not in state.scan_id
assert "payment-service" not in state.scan_id

print("PASS: scan identity is path-independent.")

scan:e71c869927b7cb35626f

PROVENANCE
target=/home/example/enterprise/payment-service

That's exactly what we want.

One deliberate limitation

This currently uses the first application_id when generating the scan ID.

That's fine for ECDAT's current repository scan model because your canonical scan currently represents a single logical application. But if we later support a single scan containing multiple independent applications, we'll upgrade this to a deterministic multi-application scan identity rather than silently pretending the first one represents all of them.

Don't solve that prematurely.

Make this surgical change

In:

backend/analysis/scan_diff.py

replace the entire _evidence_projection() function with:

Python
Run
def _evidence_projection(
    artifact: Dict[str, Any],
    evidence_by_id: Dict[str, Dict[str, Any]] | None = None,
) -> List[Dict[str, Any]]:
    """
    Build semantic evidence observations for temporal comparison.

    Evidence IDs are observation-specific and may change when source
    locations move. Therefore they are not sufficient to determine
    whether evidence itself changed.

    The projection preserves:
    - evidence text
    - surrounding context
    - source file
    - source line

    Location changes can therefore be classified separately from
    actual evidence-content changes.
    """
    evidence_by_id = evidence_by_id or {}

    projections = []

    for evidence_id in (
        artifact.get("evidence_ids") or []
    ):
        evidence = evidence_by_id.get(
            str(evidence_id)
        )

        if not evidence:
            projections.append(
                {
                    "evidence_id": str(evidence_id),
                }
            )
            continue

        projections.append(
            {
                "text": str(
                    evidence.get("text") or ""
                ),
                "context": evidence.get(
                    "context"
                ) or [],
                "file": str(
                    evidence.get("file") or ""
                ),
                "line": int(
                    evidence.get("line") or 0
                ),
            }
        )

    return sorted(
        projections,
        key=lambda value: (
            value.get("text", ""),
            str(value.get("context", "")),
            value.get("file", ""),
            value.get("line", 0),
        ),
    )

But don't stop there. build_scan_diff() currently doesn't have the persisted evidence indexed.

We need that too.

Inside build_scan_diff(), immediately after:

Python
Run
    after_artifacts = (
        to_scan.get("canonical_artifacts") or []
    )

add:

Python
Run
    before_evidence_by_id = {
        str(evidence.get("evidence_id")): evidence
        for evidence in (
            from_scan.get("evidence") or []
        )
        if evidence.get("evidence_id")
    }

    after_evidence_by_id = {
        str(evidence.get("evidence_id")): evidence
        for evidence in (
            to_scan.get("evidence") or []
        )
        if evidence.get("evidence_id")
    }

Then replace this:

Python
Run
        before_evidence = _evidence_projection(before)
        after_evidence = _evidence_projection(after)

                        "from_artifact_id": before_id,

with:

Python
Run
        before_evidence = _evidence_projection(
            before,
            before_evidence_by_id,
        )

        after_evidence = _evidence_projection(
            after,
            after_evidence_by_id,
        )

        if before_evidence != after_evidence:
            before_semantic = sorted(
                {
                    (
                        item.get("text", ""),
                        str(
                            item.get("context", "")
                        ),
                    )
                    for item in before_evidence
                }
            )

            after_semantic = sorted(
                {
                    (
                        item.get("text", ""),
                        str(
                            item.get("context", "")
                        ),
                    )
                    for item in after_evidence
                }
            )

            if before_semantic == after_semantic:
                evidence_relocated.append(
                    {
                        "from_artifact_id": before_id,
                        "to_artifact_id": after_id,
                        "match_method": match_method,
                        "before": before_evidence,
                        "after": after_evidence,
                        "semantic_change": "evidence_relocated",
                    }
                )
            else:
                evidence_changed.append(
                    {
                        "from_artifact_id": before_id,
                        "to_artifact_id": after_id,
                        "match_method": match_method,
                        "before": before_evidence,
                        "after": after_evidence,

One subtle point: I'm intentionally comparing text + context as the evidence semantics while allowing file and line to differ. That's what makes “the same cryptographic observation moved three lines upward” a relocation rather than a semantic change.

Yep. Now we have the answer.

The evidence records prove the TLS evidence actually changed because the MD5 deletion shifted the source lines:

	Scan A	Scan C
TLS artifact	same artifact:55c12...	same artifact:55c12...
TLS line	27	24
TLS evidence ID	evidence:82cb...	evidence:020e...
TLS text	same	same
TLS context	lines 25–29	lines 22–26

So the diff engine reporting evidence_changed is not ideal. Semantically this is another evidence relocation, not an evidence-content change.

We already defined the correct principle:

Artifact identity ≠ source location
Evidence identity = observation/location

Therefore deleting three lines above TLS should produce:

MD5       → REMOVED ARTIFACT
TLS       → UNCHANGED ARTIFACT
TLS       → EVIDENCE RELOCATED

not:

TLS → evidence_changed
This is worth fixing

The bug is in the diff classification logic, not the identity model.

Our current methodology says:

“location movement is relocation; non-relocation reference changes are evidence changes”

But the engine apparently isn't recognizing that the evidence text itself is unchanged while line/context moved.

That's a real correctness issue. Better to fix it now than build verification on top of a slightly lying diff engine.

Yes. I went through the attached competitive-research material, and I would change our ECDAT strategy based on it. The research is useful because it kills several assumptions that would otherwise make us build a technically impressive but strategically ordinary CBOM product. 

Pasted markdown

My main takeaway

We should stop thinking:

“How do we make ECDAT a better crypto scanner?”

and start thinking:

“How do we make ECDAT the system that reasons about cryptographic change and proves whether the intended change actually happened?”

That distinction is huge.

What I would lock into ECDAT

Our core should become:

DISCOVER
   ↓
EVIDENCE
   ↓
UNDERSTAND PURPOSE
   ↓
ASSESS RISK
   ↓
ADD BUSINESS CONTEXT
   ↓
MAKE DECISION
   ↓
PLAN MIGRATION
   ↓
MODEL IMPACT
   ↓
DEFINE EXPECTED STATE
   ↓
RESCAN
   ↓
COMPARE ACTUAL STATE
   ↓
VERIFY

We already have a surprisingly good portion of the bottom half.

The work we've just completed — stable identity → historical scans → semantic diff → verification — is therefore not something to throw away. It's actually becoming more important given the competitive landscape.

1. Our current temporal work was absolutely the right move

The research specifically says generic scan diff isn't enough.

That's exactly what we discovered experimentally.

We now have:

Scan A
  ↓
artifact identity
  ↓
Scan B
  ↓
semantic comparison
  ↓
evidence relocation
  ↓
risk/MOSCA changes
  ↓
verification

And we proved:

MD5 removed
      ↓
artifact removed
      ↓
TLS merely moved
      ↓
TLS evidence relocated
      ↓
verification = VERIFIED

That's a much better foundation than simply comparing CBOM JSON files.

So I would KEEP this architecture.

Don't redesign it.

2. The biggest architectural addition: Decision

The attached research makes this especially clear.

We have:

CryptoArtifact
Evidence
Risk
MOSCA
Recommendation
Migration
Verification

But we're missing an explicit decision object.

I think we should add:

CryptographicDecision

Something like:

Decision
├── decision_id
├── artifact_id
├── decision_type
├── decision_status
├── rationale
├── evidence_ids
├── risk_snapshot
├── business_context_snapshot
├── algorithm_status_snapshot
├── selected_migration_option
├── expected_state
├── created_at
└── supersedes

Why?

Because:

Recommendation ≠ Decision.

A recommendation says:

“ML-DSA may be suitable.”

A decision says:

“For this artifact, we selected ML-DSA because the artifact performs digital signatures, the application is business-critical, the current algorithm is quantum-vulnerable, and the migration option is compatible with the deployment constraints.”

That is much more enterprise-grade.

3. Algorithm intelligence needs to become dynamic

This is probably the most important thing I took from the HAWK development described in the research.

We currently think somewhat like:

RSA → HIGH
ECDSA → HIGH
ML-DSA → GOOD

That's insufficient long-term.

ECDAT should eventually know:

Algorithm
├── security status
├── standardization status
├── lifecycle status
├── current recommendation status
├── known attacks
├── confidence
├── source
├── effective date
├── last reviewed
├── superseded_by
└── affected decisions

Then something like:

ALGORITHM STATUS CHANGE
        ↓
ML-DSA status changes
        ↓
affected decisions discovered
        ↓
migration plans impacted
        ↓
risk recalculated
        ↓
operator notified

That gives ECDAT crypto-agility at the intelligence layer.

And that is much more interesting than hard-coding another PQC recommendation table.

4. Don't compete scanner-for-scanner

This is where AWS CryptaMap changes the strategy.

The research shows that serious projects can already cover enormous inventories, particularly cloud infrastructure. 

Pasted markdown

So I would architect ECDAT around:

             EVIDENCE PROVIDERS
                    │
       ┌────────────┼────────────┐
       ↓            ↓            ↓
    Source        Cloud        Certs
    Scanner       APIs          PKI
       ↓            ↓            ↓
   Dependencies  Runtime      Network
       └────────────┼────────────┘
                    ↓
              ECDAT CANONICAL
                 MODEL
                    ↓
                ANALYTICS

In other words:

ECDAT doesn't need to personally discover everything on Earth.

It needs to be able to consume evidence from everything.

That's a much better enterprise architecture.

5. AST should still be added

The research mentioning PostQuant is important.

Our scanner is currently primarily pattern/API based.

We shouldn't add AST because:

“Competitor has AST.”

We add it because AST gives us better evidence.

The pipeline should become:

AST
 ↓
operation
 ↓
cryptographic primitive
 ↓
purpose inference
 ↓
confidence
 ↓
artifact

For example:

rsa.generate_private_key()

isn't just:

RSA

We want:

RSA
purpose = key_generation
operation = generate_private_key
confidence = HIGH
evidence = AST call expression

That's much more valuable.

6. Business Context should become causal

Our Business Context work should stay.

But don't make:

business_criticality = HIGH

just another dashboard field.

We want:

Business Context
      ↓
Application
      ↓
Cryptographic Exposure
      ↓
Potential Impact
      ↓
Migration Priority

Example:

Payment API
   ↓
RSA-2048
   ↓
digital signature
   ↓
HNDL exposure
   ↓
customer transaction service
   ↓
financial impact = HIGH
   ↓
operational criticality = CRITICAL
   ↓
MOSCA = AT_RISK
   ↓
MIGRATION PRIORITY = P0

Now the business context actually changes the decision.

That's what makes it analytical rather than decorative.

7. Migration should become a state machine

This is probably the biggest next engineering target.

Instead of:

Migration Option

we should evolve toward:

Migration
├── planned
├── approved
├── in_progress
├── partially_migrated
├── migrated
├── verification_pending
├── verified
├── failed
├── blocked
└── superseded

Then ECDAT can tell the difference between:

“Someone recommended ML-DSA.”

and:

“The organization approved migration, implementation occurred, the old RSA artifact disappeared, the replacement was detected, and post-migration verification succeeded.”

That is enterprise migration assurance.

8. Expected State is the missing bridge

This is the piece I'd put immediately before Verification.

Something like:

Migration Plan
      ↓
Expected State

Example:

EXPECTED

artifact:
RSA-2048
purpose:
digital_signature

should become:

ML-DSA
purpose:
digital_signature

old artifact:
ABSENT

new artifact:
PRESENT

TLS dependency:
PQC-compatible

Then:

actual scan
     ↓
semantic diff
     ↓
expected vs observed

Result:

VERIFIED
PARTIAL
FAILED
BLOCKED
INCONCLUSIVE

That's much stronger than our current verification which primarily asks:

“Did the old artifact disappear or improve?”

We eventually want:

“Did reality match the migration intent?”

9. Runtime evidence should be a future evidence source, not a separate subsystem

The research's CipherIQ/CryptaMap findings are useful here.

Don't architect:

static scanner
+
runtime scanner

as two products.

Architect:

EvidenceSource

with types:

SOURCE_CODE
DEPENDENCY
CERTIFICATE
CONFIGURATION
CLOUD
NETWORK
RUNTIME
BINARY
CONTAINER

Then all produce the same canonical evidence structure.

So later:

Source says RSA
Runtime says ECDH
Certificate says RSA

becomes:

CONTRADICTORY_CRYPTO_STATE

That's powerful.

10. We should explicitly model contradictions

This is an idea I'd add to the roadmap.

Imagine:

SOURCE
ML-KEM detected

CONFIG
ML-KEM configured

RUNTIME
RSA observed

CERTIFICATE
RSA certificate active

A normal CBOM might list four facts.

ECDAT should say:

⚠ MIGRATION CONTRADICTION

Expected:
ML-KEM

Observed:
RSA

Confidence:
HIGH

Likely cause:
legacy runtime path / certificate dependency

That is exactly the kind of reasoning layer that can distinguish ECDAT.

11. Blast radius should be dependency-driven

The research is right that migration simulation is increasingly becoming a competitive direction.

So don't build a pretty “impact score.”

Build actual graph traversal:

Artifact
 ↓
Component
 ↓
Application
 ↓
Dependency
 ↓
Certificate
 ↓
Endpoint
 ↓
Business Service

Then:

Change RSA certificate

could produce:

Affected:
├── 3 services
├── 7 endpoints
├── 2 applications
├── 1 certificate chain
├── 4 deployment environments
└── 1 critical business process

Now blast radius is derived, not guessed.

12. What I would NOT build right now

Based on the research, I'd explicitly deprioritize:

❌ Another generic CBOM viewer
❌ Fancy crypto donut charts
❌ Yet another PQC readiness score
❌ Generic compliance dashboard
❌ Generic “AI assistant”
❌ ZK proofs just because a project mentions them
❌ Huge cloud scanner before our analytical model is ready

Especially the ZK stuff.

The research describes the digital-twin project as scaffold-stage. We don't need to chase every flashy concept.

                 ┌─────────────────────────┐

                              ↓
                   ┌────────────────────┐

And the product thesis becomes:

ECDAT is an evidence-linked cryptographic migration assurance platform that connects discovery, risk, business context, migration decisions and post-migration verification across heterogeneous evidence sources.

That's the sentence I'd build the rest of the system around.

My recommended priority from here

We've just finished temporal identity/diff + verification foundation.

Next I'd do:

1. Verification identity cleanup

So yes — the material changes our strategy, but it does not invalidate the work we've done.

Quite the opposite: the work we've just finished is one of the pieces that now looks strategically strongest. 

Pasted markdown

Sources

Yes. I re-cross-checked the architecture against fresh 2026 sources, NIST's current PQC/migration material, the AWS CryptaMap implementation, and the two migration/LLM research papers. The conclusion is actually stronger than before.

Cross-verdict

Our architecture is directionally right, but I would make one important correction:

AI should not merely sit “around” ECDAT. It should become a formal Intelligence Layer with tightly bounded authority over the deterministic ECDAT core.

That is the realistic enterprise architecture.

NIST itself frames PQC migration around discovery → inventory → risk/prioritization → migration, and explicitly discusses automated discovery of where/how public-key cryptography is used and systematic migration approaches. 
NCCoE
+1

NIST's current crypto-agility work also defines the broader problem as being able to replace/adapt cryptography across applications, protocols, software, hardware, firmware and infrastructure while keeping operations running. 
NIST
+1

So we're not inventing the wrong problem.

Why this survives the research
1. Discovery is not our moat

This is now confirmed even more strongly.

AWS CryptaMap currently advertises 99 AWS service scanners, org-wide account/region aggregation, CycloneDX 1.7 CBOM, PQC roadmap, MITRE PQCC workbook and Security Hub output. 
GitHub

So:

ECDAT > CryptaMap because we scan 120 things

would be a stupid battle.

Instead:

CryptaMap
     │
     ▼
AWS Evidence Provider
     │
     ▼
          ECDAT
     Canonical Graph

Consume specialized inventories. Reason across them.

That's the scalable architecture.

2. AI absolutely belongs

The research is particularly interesting here.

The June 2026 study found that LLMs can assist PQC migration, but reliability depends heavily on domain-specific tuning; it achieved strong results on controlled code fragments while showing limitations on larger repositories with complex dependencies and cross-module interactions. The authors explicitly recommend coupling AI migration with automated verification and dependency-aware validation. 
arXiv

Then the August 2026 Agentic Cryptographic Debt study makes the warning even sharper: across 56 repository-level migration runs, the tested agents failed to produce successful migrations even when conforming primitives were available, and often failed to correctly report blockers. 
Preprints

That gives us a very clean architectural rule:

                 AI
                  │
        ┌─────────┴─────────┐
        │                   │
     PROPOSE              EXPLAIN
        │                   │
        ▼                   ▼
   ECDAT CORE          ECDAT EVIDENCE
        │
        ▼
      VERIFY
        │
        ▼
      TRUST
AI gets to:

investigate

summarize

correlate

explain

generate migration candidates

generate remediation plans

translate natural language into graph queries

identify suspicious patterns

draft reports

assist engineers

AI does not independently decide:

“this artifact definitely exists”

“this algorithm is safe”

“migration succeeded”

“risk is CRITICAL”

“this replacement is compatible”

Those should come from evidence + deterministic engines + knowledge sources.

3. Verification isn't a side feature

This research actually validates the work we've already done.

The dangerous scenario is:

AI migration
     ↓
code compiles
     ↓
developer thinks migration succeeded
     ↓
crypto exposure remains

The research calls out exactly this gap between apparent completion and realised security effect. 
Preprints

ECDAT should therefore enforce:

Migration Proposed
       ↓
Expected State Created
       ↓
Implementation
       ↓
Rescan
       ↓
Semantic Diff
       ↓
Expected vs Observed
       ↓
Verification
       ↓
Assurance

This isn't theoretical fluff.

This is precisely where AI-assisted migration needs an external assurance mechanism.

4. Knowledge must be dynamic

This also survives verification.

NIST's PQC program is not frozen. Its current publication set includes finalized standards plus continuing work on additional signatures, key generation and KEM guidance. 
NIST Computer Security Resource Center
+1

And NIST's current PQC page documents the July 2026 HAWK withdrawal after an AI-assisted cryptanalytic discovery, while explicitly distinguishing it from finalized ML-KEM and ML-DSA. 
NIST

Therefore:

Algorithm
    │
    ├── current status
    ├── standards
    ├── security status
    ├── implementation status
    ├── compatibility
    ├── effective date
    └── evidence sources

is much better than:

Python
Run
if algorithm == "RSA":
    risk = "HIGH"

Our knowledge layer should eventually be versioned and time-aware.

5. Business context belongs inside the causal graph

Not as a decorative field.

This:

Application
 └── criticality = HIGH

is weak.

This:

Business Service
      ↓
Customer / Financial Data
      ↓
Data Lifetime
      ↓
Application
      ↓
Cryptographic Artifact
      ↓
Quantum Exposure
      ↓
Migration Complexity
      ↓
Business Priority

is useful.

And it aligns with NIST's migration framing: inventory isn't the end goal; inventories support risk management and prioritization decisions. 
NCCoE

6. The biggest thing I'd add: an explicit Decision Layer

This is the one architectural component I would now make mandatory.

We currently have:

Risk
Recommendation
Migration
Verification

But:

Recommendation ≠ Decision.

A recommendation is:

“Consider migrating RSA to X.”

A decision is:

Decision
├── artifact
├── selected migration
├── rationale
├── evidence
├── business context
├── risk snapshot
├── algorithm-status snapshot
├── approver
├── timestamp
├── expected state
└── supersedes

That turns ECDAT into an actual enterprise system.

7. The final lifecycle

After this second research pass, I'd lock this as the ECDAT product lifecycle:

                    DISCOVER
                       ↓
                    NORMALIZE
                       ↓
                    EVIDENCE
                       ↓
                  UNDERSTAND
                       ↓
          ┌────────────┴────────────┐
          ↓                         ↓
      RISK ENGINE             KNOWLEDGE ENGINE
          │                         │
          └────────────┬────────────┘
                       ↓
                   PRIORITIZE
                       ↓
                    DECIDE
                       ↓
                  MIGRATION
                       ↓
                EXPECTED STATE
                       ↓
                  IMPLEMENT
                       ↓
                    RESCAN
                       ↓
                 SEMANTIC DIFF
                       ↓
             EXPECTED vs OBSERVED
                       ↓
                  VERIFY
                       ↓
                  ASSURE
                       ↓
                    DRIFT
                       │
                       └────────────→ RESCAN

And AI touches almost every stage, but through controlled interfaces:

AI
│
├── Discovery assistance
├── Evidence interpretation
├── Natural-language investigation
├── Risk explanation
├── Migration candidate generation
├── Dependency investigation
├── Blast-radius explanation
├── Decision briefing
├── Remediation generation
├── Verification analysis
├── Report generation
└── Continuous analyst/copilot
So, after cross-verification:
KEEP

Canonical model

Evidence-linked graph

Risk + quantum risk

MOSCA

Business Context

Temporal history

Semantic diff

Verification

Topology

Migration intelligence

ADD

Cryptographic Decision

Expected State

Migration State Machine

Algorithm/Standards Knowledge Ledger

Dependency + Blast Radius

Contradiction Detection

AI Intelligence Layer

AI Evidence Gateway

Evidence-provider architecture

DON'T CHASE

scanner-count competition

generic chatbot

generic CBOM generation

graph-as-a-feature

autonomous unverified code migration

blockchain just because the SIH theme contains blockchain

flashy AI that cannot prove its claims

And there's a particularly nice validation from NIST: its own Migration to PQC project has separate cryptographic discovery and interoperability/performance workstreams, while its crypto-agility work focuses on making cryptographic replacement operationally sustainable. 
NCCoE
+1

So our direction is not:

“Let's build a cool AI crypto scanner.”

It is:

“Let's build an evidence-backed cryptographic migration operating system, with AI acting as the analyst and copilot, while deterministic engines provide the security truth and verification.”

That is the architecture I'd stop debating and start implementing.

Run this whole command:

Bash
python - <<'PY'
from pathlib import Path

p = Path("backend/analysis/verification.py")
text = p.read_text()

old1 = '''        verification_id = (
            f"verification:{artifact_id}:"
            f"{to_scan.get('scan_id', 'unknown')}"
        )'''

new1 = '''        verification_id = build_verification_id(
            from_scan_id=from_scan.get("scan_id", "unknown"),
            to_scan_id=to_scan.get("scan_id", "unknown"),
            artifact_id=artifact_id,
            migration_option_id=migration_option_id,
            replacement_artifact_id=replacement_artifact_id,
        )'''

old2 = '''    verification_id = (
        f"verification:{artifact_id}:"
        f"{to_scan.get('scan_id', 'unknown')}"
    )'''

new2 = '''    verification_id = build_verification_id(
        from_scan_id=from_scan.get("scan_id", "unknown"),
        to_scan_id=to_scan.get("scan_id", "unknown"),
        artifact_id=artifact_id,
        migration_option_id=migration_option_id,
        replacement_artifact_id=replacement_artifact_id,
    )'''

if text.count(old1) != 1:
    raise SystemExit("Expected exactly one first verification-ID block.")

if text.count(old2) != 1:
    raise SystemExit("Expected exactly one second verification-ID block.")

text = text.replace(old1, new1, 1)
text = text.replace(old2, new2, 1)

marker = "from model.schema import VerificationState"

if "from model.identity import build_verification_id" not in text:
    if marker not in text:
        raise SystemExit("Could not find schema import marker.")
    text = text.replace(
        marker,
        marker + "\nfrom model.identity import build_verification_id",
        1,
    )

p.write_text(text)

print("VERIFICATION IDENTITY UPDATED")
PY

The important distinction

We have already built something valuable:

ECDAT has a canonical analytical backbone.

Discovery → evidence → artifact → risk → MOSCA → recommendation → migration → history → verification is no longer just a PowerPoint architecture.

But some pieces are currently:

implemented engine → production hardening needed

while others are:

architectural contract → real engine still needs to be built.

That's completely normal at this stage.

Where I think we should go now

I would not add random features.

We should turn the foundation into a genuinely strong system by building the missing intelligence in this order:

The biggest gaps

If we're being brutally honest, the next serious engineering work is:

Expected State

What exactly should the system look like after migration?

Old artifact absent?

New algorithm present?

Certificate replaced?

Dependency compatibility preserved?

Dependency graph

Artifact → library → component → application → service → certificate → endpoint → business service.

Blast radius

“If I replace this RSA usage, what breaks?”

Decision engine

Recommendation ≠ decision.

Incorporate risk, business criticality, latency, compatibility, cost, standards status, dependencies.

Crypto Knowledge Layer

Algorithm lifecycle/status.

PQC standards.

Compatibility.

Vendor/library support.

Recommendation freshness.

Stale recommendation invalidation.

Contradiction engine

Source says ML-DSA.

Dependency says old crypto library.

Certificate is still RSA.

Runtime says RSA.

ECDAT should scream.

planned
   ↓

   ↓

   ↓

Rescan:

VERIFIED

What v0.5 will actually contain
1. Algorithm knowledge

Not just names.

Algorithm
├── canonical identity
├── aliases
├── family
├── primitive
├── key establishment / signature / encryption / hash
├── classical security
├── quantum posture
├── approved / deprecated / legacy / candidate / withdrawn
├── key-size/security-strength information
└── lifecycle dates
2. Standards knowledge

For example:

ML-KEM
 ├── FIPS 203
 ├── standard status
 ├── publication date
 ├── source authority
 └── evidence/provenance

No fabricated “approved” status.

3. Migration relationships

Not simplistic replacement mappings.

RSA
 ├── purpose=signature
 │      → ML-DSA
 │      → SLH-DSA
 │      → hybrid candidate
 │
 └── purpose=key-establishment
        → ML-KEM
        → hybrid candidate

The artifact's purpose matters.

4. Compatibility model

Eventually:

Algorithm
   ↓
Protocol
   ↓
Library
   ↓
Version
   ↓
Platform
   ↓
Capability

So ECDAT can distinguish:

“Cryptographically desirable”

from:

“Actually deployable in this environment.”

5. Temporal knowledge

This is critical.

Knowledge becomes:

Known at T1
      ↓
status changes
      ↓
Known at T2

That allows us to determine whether an existing recommendation is stale.

6. Provenance

Every important knowledge assertion needs:

source
authority
publication date
effective date
retrieved/verified date
knowledge version
confidence

This is especially important because the PQC ecosystem is actively changing. NIST's current page records, for example, the 2026 withdrawal of HAWK while explicitly noting that finalized ML-KEM and ML-DSA are unaffected. 
NIST

7. Knowledge resolution engine

Given an ECDAT artifact:

RSA
purpose = signature
key_size = 2048

the resolver should produce structured knowledge:

algorithm_status
quantum_status
security_status
applicable_standards
migration_candidates
constraints
knowledge_version
evidence
freshness
8. Recommendation invalidation

This is one of the features I particularly want.

If:

Recommendation R1
generated using Knowledge K17

and the knowledge changes:

K17 → K18

ECDAT can determine:

STALE
REVALIDATION_REQUIRED

rather than silently pretending the old recommendation is still authoritative.

9. Deterministic decision inputs

The Knowledge Layer must feed:

Risk
MOSCA
Business Impact
Recommendation
Decision
Migration
Expected State
Verification

It should not independently make an opaque AI-style decision.

10. Complete API integration

The web app must eventually be able to query:

algorithm knowledge
standards
status
compatibility
migration candidates
knowledge version
freshness
provenance

without knowing how the knowledge engine works internally.

Important distinction: the knowledge registry we just created is data-driven, not if algorithm == ... decision logic. The next step removes those hardcoded recommendation branches completely.

Replace backend/analysis/recommendations.py
Bash
cd ~/ECDAT/ECDAT1

cat > backend/analysis/recommendations.py <<'PY'
"""
ECDAT recommendation engine.

Decision logic is knowledge-driven.

The engine does NOT contain algorithm-specific migration rules.
Cryptographic relationships, lifecycle state, applicability and
compatibility come from backend/knowledge.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import Any, Dict, List, Optional

from knowledge.service import KnowledgeService


SERVICE = KnowledgeService()


def _artifact_algorithm(artifact: Dict[str, Any]) -> str:
    return str(
        artifact.get("algorithm")
        or artifact.get("algorithm_name")
        or "UNKNOWN"
    ).strip()


def _artifact_purpose(
    artifact: Dict[str, Any],
) -> Optional[str]:
    purpose = artifact.get("purpose")

    if isinstance(purpose, dict):
        purpose = purpose.get("value")

    if purpose:
        return str(purpose).strip().lower()

    return None


def _risk_level(
    artifact: Dict[str, Any],
) -> str:
    risk = artifact.get("risk") or {}

    if isinstance(risk, dict):
        quantum = risk.get("quantum") or {}

        if isinstance(quantum, dict):
            level = quantum.get("level")
            if level:
                return str(level).upper()

    return "UNKNOWN"


def _priority(
    artifact: Dict[str, Any],
    lifecycle_status: str,
) -> str:
    risk = _risk_level(artifact)

    if lifecycle_status in {
        "withdrawn",
        "deprecated",
        "legacy",
    }:
        return "CRITICAL"

    if risk == "CRITICAL":
        return "CRITICAL"

    if risk == "HIGH":
        return "HIGH"

    if risk == "MEDIUM":
        return "MEDIUM"

    if risk == "LOW":
        return "LOW"

    return "REVIEW"


def _candidate_text(
    candidate,
    compatibility_status: Optional[str] = None,
) -> str:
    suffix = ""

    if compatibility_status == "unsupported":
        suffix = " Current target compatibility is unsupported."

    elif compatibility_status == "conditional":
        suffix = " Current target compatibility is conditional."

    elif compatibility_status == "unknown":
        suffix = " Target compatibility is not established."

    if candidate.hybrid:
        return (
            f"Evaluate the hybrid migration relationship "
            f"{candidate.source_algorithm} → {candidate.target_algorithm}."
            f"{suffix}"
        )

    return (
        f"Evaluate migration from "
        f"{candidate.source_algorithm} to "
        f"{candidate.target_algorithm}."
        f"{suffix}"
    )


def get_recommendation(
    artifact: Dict[str, Any],
    *,
    knowledge_service: Optional[KnowledgeService] = None,
    as_of: Optional[str] = None,
) -> str:
    """
    Return the primary explainable recommendation.

    This function intentionally contains no algorithm-specific branches.
    """

    service = knowledge_service or SERVICE

    algorithm = _artifact_algorithm(artifact)
    purpose = _artifact_purpose(artifact)

    result = service.resolve(
        algorithm,
        purpose=purpose,
        as_of=as_of,
    )

    if result.algorithm is None:
        return (
            "Cryptographic knowledge resolution is unresolved for "
            f"'{algorithm}'. Perform manual review before selecting "
            "a migration candidate."
        )

    knowledge = result.algorithm

    if knowledge.lifecycle_status in {
        "withdrawn",
        "deprecated",
        "legacy",
    }:
        candidates = result.migrations

        if candidates:
            target_names = ", ".join(
                sorted(
                    {
                        candidate.target_algorithm
                        for candidate in candidates
                    }
                )
            )

            return (
                f"{knowledge.name} has lifecycle status "
                f"{knowledge.lifecycle_status}. "
                f"Evaluate the knowledge-linked migration candidates: "
                f"{target_names}."
            )

        return (
            f"{knowledge.name} has lifecycle status "
            f"{knowledge.lifecycle_status}. "
            "No validated migration candidate was resolved; "
            "perform manual review."
        )

    if knowledge.quantum_posture == "quantum_vulnerable":
        if result.migrations:
            candidate = result.migrations[0]

            return _candidate_text(
                candidate
            )

        return (
            f"{knowledge.name} is classified as "
            "quantum-vulnerable, but no applicable migration "
            "relationship was resolved for the observed purpose."
        )

    if knowledge.quantum_posture == "quantum_dependent":
        return (
            f"{knowledge.name} requires parameter- and purpose-aware "
            "quantum security assessment rather than automatic "
            "replacement."
        )

    if knowledge.quantum_posture == "quantum_resistant":
        return (
            f"{knowledge.name} is currently classified as "
            "quantum-resistant according to the active knowledge "
            "snapshot. Continue validating implementation and "
            "protocol compatibility."
        )

    return (
        f"Knowledge resolution for {knowledge.name} does not establish "
        "a deterministic migration action. Perform contextual review."
    )


def build_recommendation(
    artifact: Dict[str, Any],
    *,
    knowledge_service: Optional[KnowledgeService] = None,
    as_of: Optional[str] = None,
) -> Dict[str, Any]:
    """
    Produce a structured recommendation suitable for the canonical model,
    APIs and future UI.

    The result includes the knowledge snapshot so recommendations can
    later be reproduced and invalidated when knowledge changes.
    """

    service = knowledge_service or SERVICE

    algorithm = _artifact_algorithm(artifact)
    purpose = _artifact_purpose(artifact)

    resolution = service.resolve(
        algorithm,
        purpose=purpose,
        as_of=as_of,
    )

    if resolution.algorithm is None:
        return {
            "status": "UNRESOLVED",
            "algorithm": algorithm,
            "purpose": purpose,
            "priority": "REVIEW",
            "text": get_recommendation(
                artifact,
                knowledge_service=service,
                as_of=as_of,
            ),
            "rationale": (
                "No canonical knowledge record matched the observed "
                "cryptographic artifact."
            ),
            "knowledge_version": service.version,
            "knowledge_hash": service.integrity_hash,
            "matched_by": None,
            "candidate_count": 0,
            "candidate_ids": [],
            "conflict_count": 0,
        }

    knowledge = resolution.algorithm

    priority = _priority(
        artifact,
        knowledge.lifecycle_status,
    )

    candidates = list(
        resolution.migrations
    )

    compatibility_states = [
        item.status
        for item in resolution.compatibility
    ]

    if "unsupported" in compatibility_states:
        compatibility_state = "unsupported"
    elif "conditional" in compatibility_states:
        compatibility_state = "conditional"
    elif "supported" in compatibility_states:
        compatibility_state = "supported"
    else:
        compatibility_state = "unknown"

    status = (
        "CONFLICT"
        if resolution.conflicts
        else "RESOLVED"
    )

    return {
        "status": status,
        "algorithm": knowledge.name,
        "purpose": purpose,
        "priority": priority,
        "text": get_recommendation(
            artifact,
            knowledge_service=service,
            as_of=as_of,
        ),
        "rationale": knowledge.notes,
        "knowledge_version": service.version,
        "knowledge_hash": service.integrity_hash,
        "matched_by": resolution.matched_by,
        "lifecycle_status": knowledge.lifecycle_status,
        "quantum_posture": knowledge.quantum_posture,
        "primitive": knowledge.primitive,
        "standards": list(
            knowledge.standards
        ),
        "candidate_count": len(candidates),
        "candidate_ids": [
            candidate.relationship_id
            for candidate in candidates
        ],
        "candidates": [
            {
                "relationship_id": candidate.relationship_id,
                "target_algorithm": candidate.target_algorithm,
                "relationship_type": candidate.relationship_type,
                "hybrid": candidate.hybrid,
                "confidence": candidate.confidence,
                "prerequisites": list(
                    candidate.prerequisites
                ),
                "constraints": list(
                    candidate.constraints
                ),
            }
            for candidate in candidates
        ],
        "compatibility": {
            "status": compatibility_state,
            "records": len(
                resolution.compatibility
            ),
        },
        "conflict_count": len(
            resolution.conflicts
        ),
        "explainability": resolution.explainability,
    }


def get_migration_candidates(
    artifact: Dict[str, Any],
    *,
    knowledge_service: Optional[KnowledgeService] = None,
    as_of: Optional[str] = None,
) -> List[Dict[str, Any]]:
    """
    Expose knowledge-derived migration candidates without embedding
    cryptographic algorithm rules in the analysis layer.
    """

    service = knowledge_service or SERVICE

    result = service.resolve(
        _artifact_algorithm(artifact),
        purpose=_artifact_purpose(artifact),
        as_of=as_of,
    )

    return [
        {
            "relationship_id": candidate.relationship_id,
            "source_algorithm": candidate.source_algorithm,
            "target_algorithm": candidate.target_algorithm,
            "relationship_type": candidate.relationship_type,
            "hybrid": candidate.hybrid,
            "confidence": candidate.confidence,
            "prerequisites": list(
                candidate.prerequisites
            ),
            "constraints": list(
                candidate.constraints
            ),
        }
        for candidate in result.migrations
    ]

Add the regression proving there are no algorithm decision branches
Bash
cat > backend/tests/test_knowledge_recommendations.py <<'PY'
import sys
from pathlib import Path

sys.path.insert(
    0,
    str(Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]),
)

from analysis.recommendations import (
    build_recommendation,
    get_migration_candidates,
    get_recommendation,
)


def test_rsa_signature_is_knowledge_driven():
    artifact = {
        "algorithm": "RSA",
        "purpose": "digital_signature",
        "risk": {
            "quantum": {
                "level": "HIGH"
            }
        },
    }

    result = build_recommendation(artifact)

    assert result["status"] == "RESOLVED"
    assert result["algorithm"] == "RSA"
    assert "ML-DSA" in {
        item["target_algorithm"]
        for item in result["candidates"]
    }

    assert result["knowledge_version"] == "0.5.0"
    assert result["knowledge_hash"]


def test_rsa_key_establishment_is_different():
    artifact = {
        "algorithm": "RSA",
        "purpose": "key_establishment",
        "risk": {
            "quantum": {
                "level": "HIGH"
            }
        },
    }

    result = build_recommendation(artifact)

    targets = {
        item["target_algorithm"]
        for item in result["candidates"]
    }

    assert "ML-KEM" in targets
    assert "ML-DSA" not in targets


def test_unknown_algorithm_does_not_invent_recommendation():
    artifact = {
        "algorithm": "FUTURE-CRYPTO-999",
        "purpose": "digital_signature",
    }

    result = build_recommendation(artifact)

    assert result["status"] == "UNRESOLVED"
    assert result["candidate_count"] == 0
    assert "manual review" in result["text"].lower()


def test_candidates_are_knowledge_records():
    artifact = {
        "algorithm": "ECDH",
        "purpose": "key_establishment",
    }

    candidates = get_migration_candidates(
        artifact
    )

    assert candidates

    for candidate in candidates:
        assert candidate["relationship_id"]
        assert candidate["target_algorithm"]
        assert candidate["confidence"]


def test_public_function_has_no_algorithm_specific_lookup_table():
    import inspect
    from analysis import recommendations

    source = inspect.getsource(
        recommendations.get_recommendation
    )

    forbidden = [
        'algorithm == "RSA"',
        'algorithm == "ECDSA"',
        'algorithm == "AES"',
        'algorithm == "SHA-1"',
        'algorithm == "MD5"',
        'algorithm == "DES"',
    ]

    for pattern in forbidden:
        assert pattern not in source

If those pass, we have crossed an important architectural line:

OLD

RSA ──if──> ML-DSA
ECDH ─if──> ML-KEM
AES  ─if──> ...
MD5  ─if──> ...


NEW

Observed Artifact
       │
       ▼
Knowledge Resolver
       │
       ├── lifecycle
       ├── quantum posture
       ├── purpose
       ├── standards
       ├── provenance
       ├── temporal validity
       ├── compatibility
       └── migration relationships
                 │
                 ▼
        Recommendation Engine

So yes: the decision engine is no longer algorithm-specific hardcoded branching. The cryptographic knowledge is externalized into the knowledge dataset/registry, and the engine resolves it.

One caveat: the seed knowledge records themselves are necessarily explicit data—you have to tell ECDAT what NIST/IETF currently say. That's fundamentally different from embedding those decisions as Python if/elif logic. The next step is making that registry load/update from versioned knowledge data rather than constructing it in registry.py, plus staleness/invalidation. That's where we take this from “dynamic engine” to genuinely maintainable knowledge infrastructure.

Why this happened

Your tests currently use plain functions:

Python
Run
def test_schema_and_registry():

Those are pytest-style tests, not unittest.TestCase methods.

So there are actually two issues:

unittest discover isn't the right runner for these tests.

The tests are intentionally written in pytest style.

Better fix: use pytest if available

Check:

Bash
python -m pytest --version

If available:

Bash
PYTHONPATH=backend python -m pytest \
  backend/tests/test_knowledge_*.py \
  -v

If pytest is not installed, don't install random dependencies yet. We can convert the suite to stdlib unittest or add pytest deliberately to the project's test dependencies.

Do not change the implementation yet. First get the tests actually executing. Ran 0 tests tells us nothing about whether the code passes.

Yes — I did the web research this time, against current primary sources.

The important findings are:

NIST's PQC material, updated August 5, 2026, still lists FIPS 203, FIPS 204, and FIPS 205 as final standards. It also lists current 2026 work around additional signatures, crypto agility, KEM guidance, and transition planning. 
NIST Computer Security Resource Center
+1

NIST's May 2026 additional-signature process advanced nine candidates to round three, so our knowledge model should not incorrectly treat every PQ candidate as standardized. 
NIST Computer Security Resource Center
+1

RFC 10024, published August 2026, formally defines the three TLS 1.3 PQ/T hybrid mechanisms:

X25519MLKEM768

SecP256r1MLKEM768

SecP384r1MLKEM1024 
IETF Datatracker
+1

RFC 10024 also explicitly distinguishes the traditional ECDHE component from ML-KEM and defines the hybrid as simultaneous use of both key-establishment mechanisms. That's exactly the kind of structured relationship ECDAT's knowledge layer needs. 
IETF Datatracker

So our direction is correct, but I would not blindly continue adding records.

The next implementation should incorporate this research into the model properly:

Algorithm
   │
   ├── lifecycle
   ├── primitive
   ├── quantum posture
   ├── security strength
   └── standards
          │
          ▼
Mechanism
   │
   ├── components
   ├── protocol applicability
   ├── hybrid semantics
   └── interoperability
          │
          ▼
Migration Relationship
   │
   ├── purpose
   ├── prerequisites
   ├── constraints
   └── evidence

That is more correct than pretending a hybrid TLS mechanism is simply another standalone algorithm.

And 21/21 passing means we can safely refactor toward that model now, rather than patching around the current implementation.

Yes. I searched the current primary sources first, and there is a better next move than blindly adding more algorithms.

NIST's current material confirms three finalized PQC standards are implementable now, while additional algorithms remain in standardization. It also explicitly says HAWK was withdrawn after a vulnerability discovery in July 2026. 
NIST
+1
 NIST's current crypto-agility publication is CSWP 39upd1, finalized June 29, 2026, and defines agility around replacing/adapting cryptography across protocols, applications, software, hardware, firmware, and infrastructure. 
NIST Computer Security Resource Center
+1

So let's make ECDAT's knowledge layer reflect state over time, not just a list of algorithms.

Slice B — external knowledge registry + snapshot integrity

We are going to move the actual knowledge records out of Python.

Target:

backend/knowledge/
├── data/
│   └── registry.json          ← actual knowledge
├── schema.py
├── registry.py                ← loader only
├── resolver.py
├── lifecycle.py
├── temporal.py
├── freshness.py
├── conflicts.py
├── compatibility.py
├── migration.py
├── provenance.py
└── service.py

The important architectural change:

                registry.json
                     │
                     ▼
              validate schema
                     │
                     ▼
             canonical ordering
                     │
                     ▼
              SHA-256 digest
                     │
                     ▼
              KnowledgeSnapshot
                     │
             ┌───────┴────────┐
             ▼                ▼
        Resolution       Recommendation
             │                │
             └───────┬────────┘
                     ▼
             snapshot recorded

That gives us reproducibility.

If the registry changes from 0.5.0 to 0.5.1, an old ECDAT analysis can still say exactly which knowledge snapshot produced its recommendation.

Now we need to change the registry from knowledge author into knowledge loader.

2. Replace registry.py
Bash
cat > backend/knowledge/registry.py <<'PY'
from __future__ import annotations

import hashlib
import json
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Any, Dict

from knowledge.schema import (
    AlgorithmKnowledge,
    CompatibilityConstraint,
    KnowledgeManifest,
    KnowledgeProvenance,
    KnowledgeRegistry,
    MigrationRelationship,
    SecurityStrength,
    StandardKnowledge,
)
from knowledge.validation import validate_registry


DATA_PATH = (
    Path(__file__).resolve().parent
    / "data"
    / "registry.json"
)


def _load_raw() -> Dict[str, Any]:
    with DATA_PATH.open(
        "r",
        encoding="utf-8",
    ) as handle:
        return json.load(handle)


def _tuple(value):
    return tuple(value or [])


def _provenance(data):
    return tuple(
        KnowledgeProvenance(
            source_id=item["source_id"],
            source_type=item["source_type"],
            authority=item["authority"],
            title=item["title"],
            uri=item["uri"],
            published_at=item.get("published_at"),
            retrieved_at=item.get("retrieved_at"),
            effective_from=item.get("effective_from"),
            effective_until=item.get("effective_until"),
        )
        for item in data
    )


def _algorithm(item):
    strength = item.get(
        "security_strength",
        {},
    )

    return AlgorithmKnowledge(
        knowledge_id=item["knowledge_id"],
        name=item["name"],
        aliases=_tuple(item.get("aliases")),
        family=item["family"],
        primitive=item["primitive"],
        purposes=_tuple(item.get("purposes")),
        lifecycle_status=item["lifecycle_status"],
        quantum_posture=item["quantum_posture"],
        security_strength=SecurityStrength(
            classical_bits=strength.get("classical_bits"),
            quantum_bits=strength.get("quantum_bits"),
            basis=strength.get("basis"),
        ),
        key_sizes=_tuple(item.get("key_sizes")),
        standards=_tuple(item.get("standards")),
        description=item["description"],
        notes=item["notes"],
        effective_from=item.get("effective_from"),
        effective_until=item.get("effective_until"),
        source_ids=_tuple(item.get("source_ids")),
        confidence=item["confidence"],
        record_version=item.get("record_version", 1),
    )


def _standard(item):
    return StandardKnowledge(
        standard_id=item["standard_id"],
        authority=item["authority"],
        identifier=item["identifier"],
        title=item["title"],
        status=item["status"],
        published_at=item.get("published_at"),
        effective_from=item.get("effective_from"),
        effective_until=item.get("effective_until"),
        related_algorithms=_tuple(
            item.get("related_algorithms")
        ),
        supersedes=_tuple(
            item.get("supersedes")
        ),
        source_ids=_tuple(
            item.get("source_ids")
        ),
        confidence=item["confidence"],
        record_version=item.get("record_version", 1),
    )


def _compatibility(item):
    return CompatibilityConstraint(
        compatibility_id=item["compatibility_id"],
        algorithm=item["algorithm"],
        target_type=item["target_type"],
        target_name=item["target_name"],
        version_min=item.get("version_min"),
        version_max=item.get("version_max"),
        status=item["status"],
        constraints=_tuple(
            item.get("constraints")
        ),
        source_ids=_tuple(
            item.get("source_ids")
        ),
        effective_from=item.get("effective_from"),
        effective_until=item.get("effective_until"),
        confidence=item["confidence"],
        record_version=item.get("record_version", 1),
    )


def _migration(item):
    return MigrationRelationship(
        relationship_id=item["relationship_id"],
        source_algorithm=item["source_algorithm"],
        target_algorithm=item["target_algorithm"],
        relationship_type=item["relationship_type"],
        applicable_purposes=_tuple(
            item.get("applicable_purposes")
        ),
        hybrid=bool(item.get("hybrid", False)),
        prerequisites=_tuple(
            item.get("prerequisites")
        ),
        constraints=_tuple(
            item.get("constraints")
        ),
        source_ids=_tuple(
            item.get("source_ids")
        ),
        effective_from=item.get("effective_from"),
        effective_until=item.get("effective_until"),
        confidence=item["confidence"],
        record_version=item.get("record_version", 1),
    )


def _canonical_hash(data: Dict[str, Any]) -> str:
    canonical = json.dumps(
        data,
        sort_keys=True,
        separators=(",", ":"),
        ensure_ascii=True,
    )

    return hashlib.sha256(
        canonical.encode("utf-8")
    ).hexdigest()


def load_registry() -> KnowledgeRegistry:
    raw = _load_raw()

    provenance = _provenance(
        raw.get("sources", [])
    )

    algorithms = tuple(
        _algorithm(item)
        for item in raw.get("algorithms", [])
    )

    standards = tuple(
        _standard(item)
        for item in raw.get("standards", [])
    )

    compatibility = tuple(
        _compatibility(item)
        for item in raw.get("compatibility", [])
    )

    migrations = tuple(
        _migration(item)
        for item in raw.get("migrations", [])
    )

    registry_hash = _canonical_hash(raw)

    manifest = KnowledgeManifest(
        schema_version=raw["schema_version"],
        knowledge_version=raw["knowledge_version"],
        generated_at=raw["generated_at"],
        registry_hash=registry_hash,
        source_count=len(provenance),
        algorithm_count=len(algorithms),
        standard_count=len(standards),
        compatibility_count=len(compatibility),
        migration_count=len(migrations),
    )

    registry = KnowledgeRegistry(
        manifest=manifest,
        algorithms=algorithms,
        standards=standards,
        compatibility=compatibility,
        migrations=migrations,
        provenance=provenance,
        conflicts=(),
    )

    validate_registry(registry)

    return registry


_DEFAULT_REGISTRY = load_registry()


def get_registry() -> KnowledgeRegistry:
    return _DEFAULT_REGISTRY


def reload_registry() -> KnowledgeRegistry:
    global _DEFAULT_REGISTRY

    _DEFAULT_REGISTRY = load_registry()

    return _DEFAULT_REGISTRY
PY
3. Important: validation currently requires provenance

Our new loader handles that, but we also need to verify every compatibility/migration source exists, not just algorithms.

Replace the validation function with this strengthened version:

Bash
python - <<'PY'
from pathlib import Path

p = Path("backend/knowledge/validation.py")
s = p.read_text()

old = '''    algorithm_names = {
        item.name.upper()
        for item in registry.algorithms
    }

    for migration in registry.migrations:
'''

new = '''    source_ids = {
        source.source_id
        for source in registry.provenance
    }

    for compatibility in registry.compatibility:
        for source_id in compatibility.source_ids:
            if source_id not in source_ids:
                raise ValueError(
                    f"{compatibility.compatibility_id}: "
                    f"unknown source {source_id}"
                )

    for migration in registry.migrations:
        for source_id in migration.source_ids:
            if source_id not in source_ids:
                raise ValueError(
                    f"{migration.relationship_id}: "
                    f"unknown source {source_id}"
                )

    algorithm_names = {
        item.name.upper()
        for item in registry.algorithms
    }

    for migration in registry.migrations:
'''

if old not in s:
    raise SystemExit("Expected validation block not found")

p.write_text(
    s.replace(old, new, 1)
)
PY

What we're aiming for

Still:

21+ tests PASS
existing ECDAT tests PASS

But architecturally we've now changed this:

❌ Python registry contains crypto facts

to:

✅ registry.json contains crypto facts
        ↓
   deterministic loader
        ↓
   validated registry
        ↓
   integrity hash
        ↓
   knowledge resolver
        ↓
   recommendation

And the web research directly informs the model instead of being decorative: NIST's current crypto-agility work emphasizes adaptable cryptographic systems across the stack, and the current PQC landscape explicitly distinguishes finalized standards from algorithms still being evaluated. 
NIST
+2
NIST Computer Security Resource Center
+2

Do not commit yet. Run the tests first. If green, the next slice is knowledge freshness + stale recommendation detection + snapshot persistence, which is where ECDAT starts becoming genuinely temporal rather than just an inventory with a crypto dictionary attached.

Perfect. We have enough. Don't change the existing API structure. The route table is clean and isolated, so we can add the knowledge API surgically. 

Pasted text

And the web check gives us one important correction to bake into the API: RFC 10024 says the three TLS hybrid groups are not equivalent—X25519MLKEM768 is marked Recommended Y, while the P-256/P-384 hybrids are N. So our API must expose those distinctions instead of returning a generic “hybrid supported” flag. 
RFC Editor

Slice C — Knowledge API
